Arithmetic finiteness of very irregular varieties
Thomas Krämer, Marco Maculan

Abstract
. We prove the Shafarevich conjecture for varieties that embed in their Albanese variety with ample normal bundle, subject to mild numerical conditions. Our proof relies on the Lawrence-Venkatesh method as in the work of Lawrence-Sawin, together with the big monodromy criterion from our work with Javanpeykar and Lehn.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11G35, 14G25 (primary), 11F80, 14D07, 14G05, 14K12 (secondary).

1.
Introduction

In this paper we obtain finiteness results à la Shafarevich for a large class of varieties over number fields, thus proving the Lang-Vojta conjecture for the moduli stacks of such varieties. One of the ingredients of the proof is the big monodromy criterion in our work with Javanpeykar and Lehn
[
JKLM23
,
KLM24
]
.

1.1.
The Shafarevich conjecture

To put our results in perspective, let us briefly recall some history. At the ICM in Stockholm in 1962, Shafarevich conjectured that over any number field there are only finitely many isomorphism classes of smooth projective curves of given genus
g\geqslant 2
that have good reduction outside a given finite set
\Sigma
of places; the latter roughly means that the discriminant of an equation for the curve is divisible at most by the primes in
\Sigma
. Since then, analogous finiteness statements for other types of varieties have become known as instances of the
Shafarevich conjecture
(not to be confused with the eponymous conjecture on holomorphic convexity of the universal cover of complex projective manifolds). The conjecture does not hold for all types of varieties, for instance it fails for genus
1
curves without rational points. The correct statement is obtained by regarding varieties with good reduction as integral points of moduli spaces.

The scarcity of integral points is the object of the
Lang-Vojta conjecture
, one of the major unsolved problems in diophantine geometry. It predicts that on an algebraic variety of log general type over a number field, the integral points are not Zariski dense. In dimension one this is the Mordell conjecture that was proven by Faltings
[
Fal83
]
: Any smooth projective curve of genus
g\geqslant 2
has only finitely many rational points. In higher dimension, the Lang-Vojta conjecture implies in particular that any hyperbolic variety over a number field has only finitely many integral points. Here ‘hyperbolic’ means that all positive-dimensional subvarieties are of log general type. By the work of Campana-Păun
[
CP15
, cor. 4.6]
, moduli spaces
1
1

1

Here we ignore issues arising from the coarseness of moduli spaces.
of canonically polarized varieties are hyperbolic in this sense. Therefore the Shafarevich conjecture for a given class of canonically polarized varieties is equivalent to the Lang-Vojta conjecture for the corresponding moduli space.

In the case of curves and abelian varieties, the Shafarevich conjecture was proven by Faltings
[
Fal83
]
to deduce the Mordell conjecture (the fact that the Shafarevich conjecture would imply the Mordell conjecture had been noticed earlier by Kodaira and Parshin). Since then, there has been only little progress on the Shafarevich conjecture as we will recall below. Recently, Lawrence and Sawin
[
LS20
]
used the method of Lawrence and Venkatesh
[
LV20
]
to verify the Shafarevich conjecture in the special case of hypersurfaces in abelian varieties. In this paper we will prove it for varieties that embed in their Albanese variety with ample normal bundle. More precisely, we prove it for the following class of varieties:

Definition
.

Let
X
be a variety over a field
k
. When
k
is algebraically closed we say that
X
is
very irregular
if it smooth, projective, connected and the Albanese morphism
X\to\Alb(X)
given by some point in
X(k)
is a closed embedding with nonzero ample normal bundle. When
k
is arbitrary, we say that
X
is very irregular if the base change to an algebraic closure of
k
is very irregular.

We confirm the Lang-Vojta conjecture for the moduli spaces of very irregular varieties.
Very irregular varieties form a very large class of canonically polarized varieties which includes for instance all smooth projective curves of genus
\geqslant 2
, all smooth complete intersections of ample divisors in an abelian variety and plenty of other examples such as Fano surfaces of lines of cubic threefolds, Schoen surfaces, etc. Higher dimensional abelian varieties and their subvarieties are hard to describe by explicit equations but have a rich geometry: In particular, subvarieties of abelian varieties with ample normal bundle have been studied by many authors, including Hartshorne
[
Har71
]
, Ran
[
Ran81
]
and Debarre
[
Deb95
]
. We chose the name ‘very irregular’ in reminiscence of the classical notion of irregular varieties, that is, smooth projective complex varieties
S
whose irregularity
h^{1}(S,\mathcal{O}_{S})=h^{0}(S,\Omega^{1}_{S})
does not vanish.

1.2.
Intrinsic results

Let
K
be a number field,
\Sigma
a finite set of places and
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
the ring of
\Sigma
-integers of
K
.

Definition
.

A very irregular variety
X
over
K
has
good reduction (outside
\Sigma
)
if there is a smooth projective scheme
\mathcal{X}
over
R:=\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
with generic fiber
X
such that the relative canonical bundle
\det\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}/R}
is relatively ample.

If such a scheme
\mathcal{X}
exists, it is unique up to isomorphism; see
section

2.5
. For surfaces our main result can be stated as follows:

Theorem A
.

Fix
c\geqslant 1
. Then up to
K
-isomorphism there are only finitely many very irregular surfaces
X
with good reduction,
c_{2}(X)=c
and
h^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\geqslant 6
.

The above finiteness statement is the first one that holds for such a large class of surfaces.
Before it, the Shafarevich conjecture for surfaces was known only in the following specific cases: Abelian surfaces
[
Fal83
]
, del Pezzo surfaces
[
Sch85
]
, K3 surfaces
[
And96
,
She97
,
Tak20
]
, Enriques surfaces
[
Tak19
]
, sextic surfaces
[
JL18
]
, fibrations of smooth curves over smooth curves
[
Jav15
]
, surfaces that geometrically are products of curves
[
JL21
]
and, under mild numerical conditions, very irregular surfaces
X
with
h^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})=3

[
LS20
]
.

To state our results in higher dimension, note that a very irregular variety is canonically polarized; unless the polarization is specified, its Hilbert polynomial will be understood to be taken with respect to the canonical bundle. Also, for a
d
-dimensional smooth variety
X
we write
\chi_{\textup{top}}(X)=c_{d}(X)
for the topological Euler characteristic of
X
and
\chi(X,\mathcal{F})
for the Euler characteristic of a coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
X
. We prove the following generalization of Faltings’ theorem:

Theorem B
.

Fix
P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
of degree
d
. Then up to
K
-isomorphism there are only finitely many very irregular varieties
X
with good reduction, Hilbert polynomial
P
,
h^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\geqslant 2d+2
and

(1.1)

(-1)^{d}\chi(X\times X,\Omega^{d}_{X\times X})\;\leqslant\;\tfrac{1}{2}\chi_{\textup{top}}(X\times X),

satisfying the numerical condition (
1.2
) below with
A=\Alb(X)
.

For
d=2
the conditions (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) are always satisfied, thus
A
follows from
B
. In higher dimension
B
covers a vast class of previously unknown cases: For instance, the inequality (
1.1
) holds for all odd
d
by
lemma

B.5
; in fact we do not know any example of a smooth subvariety of an abelian variety for which it fails. The condition (
1.2
) is only needed when
X
is symmetric in the sense of
section

1.3
, and it is empty for
h^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\geqslant 4d+2
. Thus we obtain:

Corollary
.

Fix
P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
of odd degree
d
. Then up to
K
-isomorphism there are only finitely many very irregular varieties
X
with good reduction, Hilbert polynomial
P
and
h^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\geqslant 4d+2
.

In dimension
\geqslant 3
, apart from Faltings’ theorem, such finiteness results were previously known only for certain hyper-Kähler varieties
[
And96
]
, flag varieties
[
JL15
]
, complete intersections of Hodge level
\leqslant 1

[
JL17
]
, certain Fano threefolds
[
JL18
]
and very irregular varieties
X
with
h^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})=\dim X+1

[
LS20
]
.

1.3.
Numerical condition

To formulate the numerical condition in
B
, let
X
be a smooth subvariety of an abelian variety
A
of dimension
g
over a field
k
.
If
k
is algebraically closed, we say that
X\subset A
is
symmetric up to translation
if there is a point
a\in A(k)
such that
X=-X+a
. If
k
is an arbitrary field, we say that
X\subset A
is symmetric up to translation if its base change to an algebraic closure of
k
is so. By the
stabilizer
of a subvariety
X\subset A
we mean the algebraic group
\Stab_{A}(X)
whose points in a
k
-scheme
S
are

\Stab_{A}(X)(S)\;=\;\{a\in A(S)\colon X_{S}+a\;=\;X_{S}\}.

When
X\subset A
has ample normal bundle, then
G:=\Stab_{A}(X)
is a finite group scheme whose order
n=|G|
divides
\chi_{\textup{top}}(X)
because
\chi_{\textup{top}}(\bar{X})=\chi_{\textup{top}}(X)/n
for the quotient variety
\bar{X}=X/G
. In
B
we require:

(1.2)

\begin{array}[]{cc}|\chi_{\textup{top}}(\bar{X})|\neq 2^{2m-1}&\begin{tabular}[]{l}\text{if\penalty\ $X$ is symmetric up to translation,}\\
\text{$d\geqslant(g-1)/4$, $m\in\{3,\dots,d\}$, and $m\equiv d$ mod $2$.}\end{tabular}\end{array}

We do not know any example of a smooth subvariety
X\subset A
with ample normal bundle for which the condition (
1.2
) fails. We take advantage of the general setup in this section to introduce two notions that will be useful later:

Definition
.

If
k
is algebraically closed, we say that
X
is

•

divisible
if
\Stab_{A}(X)\neq 0
;

•

a
product
if there are smooth subvarieties
X_{1},X_{2}\subset A
with
\dim X_{i}>0
such that the sum morphism
X_{1}\times X_{2}\to A
is a closed embedding with image
X
.

If
k
is an arbitrary field, we say that
X
is divisible resp. a product if its base change to an algebraic closure of
k
is so.

Note that if the Albanese morphism
X\to\Alb(X)
given by some
x\in X(k)
is a closed immersion with ample normal bundle, then
X
is not a product.

1.4.
Extrinsic result

We will deduce
B
from a general result about subvarieties of arbitrary abelian varieties. Let
A
be an abelian variety of dimension
g
over
K
and let
L
be an ample line bundle on
A
. Recall that
A
over
K
has good reduction if it is the generic fiber of an abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}
over
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
. Then the abelian scheme is unique up to isomorphism.

Definition
.

A subvariety
X\subset A
has
embedded good reduction
if the Zariski closure of
X
in
\mathcal{A}
is smooth over
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
.

When
X
is very irregular and
A=\Alb(X)
this notion of good reduction is more restrictive than the previous one. In
[
LS20
]
Lawrence and Sawin prove that up to translation, there are only finitely many hypersurfaces
X\subset A
algebraically equivalent to
L
and with embedded good reduction. In the opposite case of subvarieties of high codimension, we show:

Theorem C
.

Fix
P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
of degree
d<(g-1)/2
. Then up to translation by points in
A(K)
there are only finitely many smooth geometrically connected subvarieties
X\subset A
over
K
with ample normal bundle, embedded good reduction, Hilbert polynomial
P
with respect to
L
which are not a product and satisfy (
1.1
) and (
1.2
).

We say that a subvariety
X\subset A
is a
geometric complete intersection of ample divisors
if its base change to an algebraic closure of
K
is a complete intersection of ample divisors. Smooth complete intersections of ample divisors in an abelian variety are never a product
[
JKLM23
, rem. 6.3]
, and if they are nondivisible, then they satisfy (
1.2
) by
[
JKLM23
, prop. 2.16, cor. 2.17]
. Hence as a direct consequence of
C
we obtain:

Corollary
.

Fix
P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
of degree
d<(g-1)/2
. Then up to translation by points in
A(K)
there exist only finitely many geometrically connected smooth subvarieties
X\subset A
with embedded good reduction, Hilbert polynomial
P
with respect to
L
, satisfying (
1.1
) and which are nondivisible geometric complete intersection of ample divisors.

The main geometric input for our proof of
C
is the Big Monodromy Criterion from
[
JKLM23
,
KLM24
]
that we recall in the next section.

1.5.
Big monodromy

In this section we work over
\mathbb{C}
. Let
S
be a smooth complex variety and
A
a complex abelian variety of dimension
g
. Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to A_{S}:=A\times S
be a morphism of complex varieties whose composite with the projection
\pr_{S}\colon A_{S}\to S
is smooth over
S
with connected fibers of dimension
d
. The situation is summarized in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{S}}

Let
\pi_{1}(A,0)
be the topological fundamental group and denote the group of its characters by

\Pi(A)=\Hom(\pi_{1}(A,0),\mathbb{C}^{\times}).

For
\chi\in\Pi(A)
let
L_{\chi}
denote the associated rank one local system on
A
. For
\underline{\chi}=(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{n})\in\Pi(A)^{n}
we consider the local system

V_{\underline{\chi}}\;:=\;R^{d}\pi_{S\ast}\pi_{A}^{*}L_{\underline{\chi}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{where}\hskip 10.00002ptL_{\underline{\chi}}\;:=\;L_{\chi_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus L_{\chi_{n}}.

For
s\in S(\mathbb{C})
let
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S,s)\to\GL(V_{\underline{\chi},s})
be the monodromy representation on the fiber
V_{\underline{\chi},s}
. The
algebraic monodromy group
of the local system
V_{\underline{\chi}}
is the Zariski closure of the image of
\rho
. By construction it is an algebraic subgroup of

\GL(V_{\chi_{1},s})\times\cdots\times\GL(V_{\chi_{n},s})\;\subset\;\GL(V_{\underline{\chi},s}).

We say
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to A_{S}
is
symmetric up to translation
if there are an automorphism
\iota
of
\mathcal{X}
as an
S
-scheme and
a\colon S\to A
such that
f(\iota(x))=a(\pi_{S}(x))-f(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
In this case Poincaré duality gives as in
[
JKLM23
, sect. 1.1]
for
i=1,\dots,n
a nondegenerate bilinear pairing

\theta_{\chi_{i},s}\colon\hskip 10.00002ptV_{\chi_{i},s}\otimes V_{\chi_{i},s}\longrightarrow L_{\chi_{i},a(s)}

which is symmetric if
d
is even, and alternating otherwise. The pairing is compatible with the monodromy operation, thus the algebraic monodromy group is contained in the orthogonal resp. symplectic group if
d
is even resp. odd. We say that we have big monodromy if the algebraic monodromy group is a big as possible. More precisely:

Definition
.

We say that
V_{\underline{\chi}}
has
big monodromy
if its algebraic monodromy group contains
G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{n}
as a normal subgroup, where
G_{i}\subset\GL(V_{\chi_{i},s})
is defined by

G_{i}\;:=\;\begin{cases}\SL(V_{\chi_{i},s})&\textup{if $\mathcal{X}$ is not symmetric up to translation},\\
\SO(V_{\chi_{i},s},\theta_{\chi_{i},s})&\textup{if $\mathcal{X}$ is symmetric up to translation and $d$ is even},\\
\Sp(V_{\chi_{i},s},\theta_{\chi_{i},s})&\textup{if $\mathcal{X}$ is symmetric up to translation and $d$ is odd}.\end{cases}

We say that
\mathcal{X}\to S

has big monodromy for most tuples of torsion characters
if, for each
n\geqslant 1
and all torsion
n
-tuples
\underline{\chi}\in\Pi(A)^{n}
outside a finite union of torsion translates of linear subvarieties, we have

(1)

R^{j}\pi_{S*}\pi_{A}^{*}L_{\underline{\chi}}=0
for
j\neq d
;

(2)

V_{\underline{\chi}}=R^{d}\pi_{S*}\pi_{A}^{*}L_{\underline{\chi}}
has big monodromy.

By a
linear subvariety
we mean a subset
\Pi(B)\subset\Pi(A^{n})
for an abelian quotient variety
A^{n}\twoheadrightarrow B
with
\dim B<n\dim A
.

By generic vanishing
[
BSS18
,
KW15
,
Sch15
]
condition (1) holds if
f
is finite and more generally in the semismall case as described in
example

B.2
. For the rest of
section

1.5
assume that
f
is a
closed embedding
. If the fiber
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}
over a geometric generic point
\bar{\eta}
of
S
is divisible or a product, then
V_{\underline{\chi}}
does not have big monodromy. There are two other obstructions to big monodromy: We say that
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}
is

•

constant up to a translation
if it is the translate of a subvariety
Y\subset A
along a point in
A(\bar{\eta})
;

•

a
symmetric power of a curve
if there is a curve
C\subset A_{S,\bar{\eta}}
such that the sum morphism
\Sym^{d}C\to A_{S,\bar{\eta}}
is an embedding with image
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}
and
d\geqslant 2
.

By
[
JKLM23
,
KLM24
]
these are the only obstructions to big monodromy:

Big Monodromy Criterion
.

Suppose
X:=\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}\subset A_{S,\bar{\eta}}
has ample normal bundle, dimension
d<(g-1)/2
, and satisfies the numerical assumption (
1.2
). Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

X
is nondivisible, not constant up to translation, not a symmetric power of a curve and not a product.

•

\mathcal{X}\to A_{S}
has big monodromy for most torsion tuples of characters.

For the proof of
C
we will combine the Big Monodromy Criterion with a new nondensity result for integral points given by
D
below.

1.6.
Nondensity of integral points

We now work again over the number field
K
as in
C
. Resetting notation, let
A
be an abelian scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
. Let
S
be a smooth separated finite type scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
with geometrically connected generic fiber, and
\mathcal{X}
a smooth
S
-scheme of finite type with geometrically connected fibers of dimension
d
. We say that a morphism of
S
-schemes
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to A_{S}
has
big monodromy for most tuples of torsion characters
if for some (equivalently, any) embedding
\sigma\colon K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
the family
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\to A_{S,\sigma}
does so. We prove the following new nondensity result for integral points, whose range of application goes far beyond families of subvarieties of abelian varieties:

Theorem D
.

Suppose that

•

\mathcal{X}\to A_{S}
has big monodromy for most tuples of torsion characters, and

•

every geometric fiber
X
of
\mathcal{X}\to S
satisfies (
1.1
).

Then
S(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
is not Zariski-dense in
S
.

Arithmetic consequences of big monodromy phenomena have a long history going back to the Deligne’s proof of the Weil conjectures
[
Del74
, sect. 5]
. Our proof of
D
will occupy sections
3
through
8
; in the rest of this section we briefly outline the main ideas (for a comparison with previous work see
section

1.7
).

Assume for simplicity
K=\mathbb{Q}
so that
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}=\mathbb{Z}[1/N]
for some integer
N\geqslant 1
. Pick a prime
p\nmid N
, let
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
be an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
be the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}
. We then have an embedding

\Gamma_{p}:=\Gal(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\;\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\;\Gamma:=\Gal(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}).

First of all, the Big Monodromy Criterion suggests to consider cohomology twisted by a sum of rank one local systems as in
[
LS20
]
. To do this suppose that we can find a character

\chi_{0}\colon\pi_{1}(A(\mathbb{C}),0)\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of order
r
with
p\nmid r
such that

(1)

\textup{H}^{i}(\mathcal{X}_{s}(\mathbb{C}),\pi_{A}^{\ast}L_{\chi})=0
for all
i\neq d
and all
\chi\in\Gamma.\chi_{0}
,
s\in S(\mathbb{C})
,

(2)

V_{\underline{\chi}}
has big monodromy for the tuple
\underline{\chi}
of all characters
\chi\in\Gamma.\chi_{0}
.

where
\pi_{A}\colon\mathcal{X}\to A
is the projection. Here the assumption (2) is unrealistic and only serves to simplify the presentation; we will explain how to bypass it in
section

8
. The orbit
\Gamma.\chi_{0}
corresponds to a direct summand

E\subset[r]_{\ast}\mathbb{Q}_{p,A}

where
[r]\colon A\to A
denotes the multiplication by
r
and
\mathbb{Q}_{p,A}
is the constant
p
-adic étale sheaf. For each
s\in S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])
the Galois representation

V_{s}:=\textup{H}^{d}_{\textup{\'{e}t}}(\mathcal{X}_{s}\times{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}},\pi^{\ast}_{A}E)

of
\Gamma
is pure of weight
d
by
[
Del74
]
. It is conjectured to be semisimple, but with our current state of knowledge a main task of this paper will be to work without assuming this. For this we consider the semisimplification filtration
V^{\bullet}_{s}
on
V_{s}
. By Faltings’ finiteness of pure global Galois representations
[
Del85
, th. 3.1]
, the image of the composite map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{filtered global}\\
\textup{Galois rep's}\end{array}\right\}/\cong}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{graded global}\\
\textup{Galois rep's}\end{array}\right\}/\cong}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(V_{s},V^{\bullet}_{s})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gr V^{\bullet}_{s}}}

is finite. Hence to show that
S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])
is not Zariski dense in
S
, it suffices to show that the fibers of the map

S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])\ni s\longmapsto\textup{isomorphism class of }\gr V^{\bullet}_{s}

are not Zariski dense.
A natural attempt would be to restrict representations to the local Galois group at a prime
\ell\neq p
with
\ell\nmid N
. To see why this does not suffice, fix an embedding
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
and consider the inclusion

\Gamma_{\ell}:=\Gal(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})\;\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\;\Gamma:=\Gal(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}).

For
s\in S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])
let
V_{s,\ell}
be the
p
-adic representation of
\Gamma_{\ell}
obtained from
V_{s}
by restriction. By proper base change in étale cohomology, the isomorphism class of
V_{s,\ell}
only depends on the image of
s
in
S(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
. In particular the set

\left\{V_{s,\ell}\mid s\in S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])\right\}/\cong

is finite, so there is no hope of proving nondensity by restricting to the subgroup
\Gamma_{\ell}
for a single
\ell\neq p
. Instead the heart of the Lawrence-Venkatesh method
[
LV20
]
lies in the insight that the restriction of
V_{s}
to
\Gamma_{p}
moves enough when
s\in S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])
varies, provided that the family
\mathcal{X}\to A_{S}
has big monodromy.

To make this precise we will apply
p
-adic Hodge theory. Recall that
p
-adic Hodge theory associates to every finite dimensional
crystalline
representation
V
of
\Gamma_{p}
a triple

D_{\textup{crys}}(V):=(V_{\textup{dR}},\varphi_{V},h^{\bullet}V)

of a finite dimensional
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector space
V_{\textup{dR}}
, an endomorphism
\varphi_{V}\in\End_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(V_{\textup{dR}})
and a filtration
h^{\bullet}V_{\textup{dR}}
of
V_{\textup{dR}}
by vector subspaces (not necessarily stable under
\varphi_{V}
). Such triples are called filtered isocrystals, but in this introduction we will informally call them
p
-adic Hodge structures. Rather than defining the functor
D_{\textup{crys}}
let us only mention what it does to the representations in question. To do so consider the vector bundle
[r]_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{A}
with the connection
\nabla
induced by the canonical derivation on
\mathcal{O}_{A}
. There is a well-defined direct summand

\mathcal{E}\subset[r]_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{A_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}}

associated to the local system
E
and stable under
\nabla
. For
s\in S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])
the representation
V_{s}
is crystalline since
\mathcal{X}_{s}
has good reduction at
p
. Then Faltings’ étale-de Rham comparison theorem
[
Fal89
, th. 5.6]
gives

D_{\textup{crys}}(V_{s})\;=\;(\textup{H}^{d}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{X}_{s}\times\mathbb{Q}_{p},\pi_{A}^{\ast}\mathcal{E}),\;\varphi_{s},\;\textup{Hodge filtration})

where
\varphi_{s}
is the endomorphism induced by the Frobenius operator on crystalline cohomology via the de Rham-crystalline comparison theorem. This should give the idea of how to prove that the representations
V_{s}
move. In a sense to be made precise below, we can identify de Rham cohomology groups for
s
and
s^{\prime}
close enough by integrating the Gauss-Manin connection. It then remains to show that the Hodge filtration moves enough, i.e. the associated period mapping has large enough image, which will be implied by the hypothesis of big monodromy.

In
sections

4
and
5
we develop a new framework for Galois representations and filtered isocrystals which permits to deal with operations such as taking the semisimplification of a representation and applying
p
-adic Hodge theory. In particular, the semisimplication filtration
V^{\bullet}_{s}
on
V_{s}
induces a filtration
D_{\textup{crys}}(V^{\bullet}_{s})
on
D_{\textup{crys}}(V_{s})
which is stable under the crystalline Frobenius. This operation is compatible with taking the associated graded, leading to the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{filtered global}\\
\textup{Galois rep's}\\
\textup{crystalline at $p$}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{graded global}\\
\textup{Galois rep's}\\
\textup{crystalline at $p$}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{filtered crystalline}\\
\textup{local Galois rep's}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{graded crystalline}\\
\textup{local Galois rep's}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{filtered $p$-adic}\\
\textup{Hodge structures}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{graded $p$-adic}\\
\textup{Hodge structures}\end{array}\right\}}}
restrict to
\scriptstyle\Gamma_{p}
graded
restrict to
\scriptstyle\Gamma_{p}
graded
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\textup{crys}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\textup{crys}}}
graded

To prove that
S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])
is not Zariski dense, it will be enough to show that the fibers of the map

S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])\ni s\longmapsto\textup{isomorphism class of $\gr D_{\textup{crys}}(V^{\bullet}_{s})$}

are not dense.
Consider the
d
-th relative de Rham cohomology group

\mathcal{V}\;:=\;\mathcal{H}_{\textup{dR}}^{d}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}/S_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}};\pi_{A}^{\ast}(\mathcal{E},\nabla))

By a careful choice of the prime
p
in the first place we may assume that
\mathcal{V}
is a vector bundle over
S_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. This vector bundle
\mathcal{V}
comes equipped with the Gauss-Manin connection that we still denote
\nabla
. Fix a point
o\in S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])
and consider the residue disk

\Omega=\{s\in S(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\mid s\equiv o\mod p\}.

Since
S(\mathbb{F}_{p})
is finite, it suffices to show that the intersection
\Omega\cap S(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])
is not Zariski dense for any such residue disk. Working on
\Omega
has the advantage that the Gauss-Manin connection can be integrated on
\Omega
: We have a natural isomorphism of
K
-vector spaces

\tau_{s}\colon\mathcal{V}_{s}=\textup{H}^{d}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{X}_{s}\times\mathbb{Q}_{p},\pi_{A}^{\ast}\mathcal{E})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{V}_{o}=\textup{H}^{d}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{X}_{o}\times\mathbb{Q}_{p},\pi_{A}^{\ast}\mathcal{E}),

see
section

8.5
.
The filtration on
\mathcal{V}_{o}
induced by the Hodge filtration on
\mathcal{V}_{s}
via
\tau_{s}
is by definition the image of
s
via the
p
-adic period mapping

\Phi_{p}\colon\hskip 10.00002pt\Omega\;\longrightarrow\;\Flag_{t}(\mathcal{V}_{o})

where
\Flag_{t}(\mathcal{V}_{o})
denotes the variety of flags on
\mathcal{V}_{o}
whose type
t
is the type of the flag underlying the Hodge filtration. Define an equivalence relation on
\Omega
by putting

s\sim s^{\prime}\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{if}\hskip 10.00002pt\gr D_{\textup{crys}}(V_{s}^{\bullet})\cong\gr D_{\textup{crys}}(V_{s^{\prime}}^{\bullet}).

The equivalence classes then give rise to certain constructible subsets
Z\subset\Flag_{t}(\mathcal{V}_{o})
, and we win as soon as we show that the preimage
\Phi_{p}^{-1}(Z)
of each of these subsets is not Zariski dense in
\Omega
. The Ax-Lindemann property of period mappings proved by Bakker-Tsimerman
[
BT19
]
, or rather its
p
-adic version given in
proposition

7.10
, says that for this it will be enough to show that

•

\Phi_{p}
has a Zariski dense image in
\Flag_{t}(\mathcal{V}_{o})
, and

•

\dim Z+\dim S_{\mathbb{Q}}\leqslant\dim\Flag_{t}(\mathcal{V}_{o})
.

We now have to pay for the careless discussion given above: The period mapping
\Phi_{p}
will
never
have a Zariski dense image in
\Flag_{t}(\mathcal{V}_{o})
, for the simple reason that we forgot all the extra structures that
\mathcal{E}
comes with. Indeed, over a finite extension
K
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
we have a splitting into a direct sum of line bundles

\mathcal{E}_{\rvert A_{K}}=\bigoplus_{\chi\in\Gamma.\chi_{0}}\mathcal{L}_{\chi}

and the connection decomposes accordingly. So over
K
the image of
\Phi_{p}
is contained in

\mathfrak{H}:=\prod_{\chi\in\Gamma.\chi_{0}}\Flag_{t_{\chi}}(\textup{H}^{d}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{X}_{s}\times K,\mathcal{L}_{\chi})).

The new framework developed in
sections

3
and
4
is flexible enough to enable to capture all the relevant extra structures on étale and de Rham cohomology. It will undoubtely have future applications in situations where the monodromy groups are not classical groups. In
proposition

4.6
we generalize Faltings’ finiteness theorem for pure global Galois representations in a way that keeps track of the extra structures. In
proposition

5.14
we show that our general framework is compatible with
p
-adic Hodge theory in the sense that the extra structures on étale cohomology are carried to those on de Rham cohomology via Faltings’ comparison theorem.

Suppose now that with all the extra structures in place, we started the above argument all over again. By assumption the family
\mathcal{X}\to A_{S}
has big monodromy with respect to the tuple of consisting of the characters in the orbit
\Gamma.\chi_{0}
. This implies that
\Phi_{p}
has a Zariski dense image in
\mathfrak{H}
. It remains to bound the dimension of the equivalence classes
Z
mentioned above. We do this in
theorem

6.4
by giving such an upper bound in terms of a combinatorial function and of the dimension of the centralizer of the crystalline Frobenius.
A crucial restriction on the dimension comes from the purity of the global Galois representation we started with: The filtration on each graded piece of
\gr D_{\textup{crys}}(V_{s}^{\bullet})
induced by the Hodge filtration on
D_{\textup{crys}}
has the same weight
d/2
as the original Hodge filtration. In the framework where extra structure is taken into account such a property carries over in a weaker form; see
section

3.5
and
proposition

5.17
. At this stage, to complete the proof we only need to bound the dimension of the centralizer of the crystalline Frobenius
\varphi_{o}
. In order to apply
theorem

6.4
we will need that this centralizer is very small compared to
\mathfrak{H}
. We will obtain this by letting
r
go to infinity. This will conclude the proof of
D
, hence of theorems
A
,
B
and
C
.

1.7.
Comparison with previous approaches

D
is new and goes far beyond the application to subvarieties of abelian varieties.
Its proof differs from that of
[
LS20
, th. 8.17]
in several aspects. The version of
p
-adic Hodge theory that we set up works over arbitrary
p
-adic fields rather than over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. This makes the proof of
D
simpler and more natural, allowing for families of subvarieties over arbitrary number fields rather than over
\mathbb{Q}
. We correct the implicit identification in
[
LS20
]
of the two algebraic groups
that arise naturally in the étale and de Rham realizations: In the relevant examples these two groups are not isomorphic and this leads to issues in particular with lemmas 5.49, 7.1 and 8.10 in loc. cit., see
remark

5.16
. We cast our theory for general reductive groups: This will permit to generalize
D
to cases where the algebraic monodromy is not a classical group, but for instance a product of classical groups, or in fact any reductive group once a suitable version of the
p
-adic Riemann-Hilbert correspondence is known.
Unlike
[
LS20
, lemmas 5.50 and 5.52]
, our approach to the semisimplification of Galois representations does not require the choice of Levi subgroups.
The absence of such a choice leads to a functorial construction, making sense of diagrams like the one on page
1.6
(see
proposition

5.14
).
In comparison with the notion of Hodge-Deligne systems from loc. cit., our approach in
section

4
keeps track of only the minimal amount of linear algebra needed. Finally, we address a few inaccuracies in
[
LV20
, lemma 2.6]
and
[
LS20
, lemma 5.49]
in the proof of Faltings’ finiteness of semisimple representations with values in arbitrary reductive groups; see
proposition

4.6
.

Conventions

A
variety
over a field
k
is a separated finite type
k
-scheme, and a
subvariety
is a closed subvariety. Actions are left actions unless said otherwise.

2.
Proof of theorems
A
,
B
and
C

In this section we explain how to deduce the theorems
A
,
B
,
C
from the main
D
.

2.1.
The Hilbert scheme

Let
A
be an abelian variety over a field
k
. Let
A
act on its Hilbert scheme
\Hilb_{A}
by translation. This action preserves the connected components. In particular for an ample line bundle
L
on
A
and any
P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
, the action preserves the projective variety

\Hilb_{A,L,P}\;\subset\;\Hilb_{A}

parametrizing the subschemes with Hilbert polynomial
P
with respect to
L
. Consider the subset

\Hilb_{A,L,P}^{\star}\subset\Hilb_{A,L,P}

consisting of the image of all geometric points
\Spec\Omega\to\Hilb_{A}
such that the corresponding subvariety
X\subset A_{\Omega}
is smooth, geometrically connected, nondivisible, not a product, with ample normal bundle, with Hilbert polynomial
P
with respect to
L
and such that (
1.2
) and (
1.1
) are satisfied for
d=\dim X
and
e=\chi_{\textup{top}}(X)
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
and let
L
be an ample line bundle on
A
.
Then the subset
\Hilb_{A,L,P}^{\star}\subset\Hilb_{A,L,P}
is constructible.

Proof.

Smoothness, geometric connectedness and ampleness are open conditions. Therefore the locus
U
where the universal family
\mathcal{X}\to\Hilb_{A}
has these properties is open. Let
G\subset A_{U}
the stabilizer of the
U
-scheme
\mathcal{X}_{U}\times A
, namely, the equalizer of the morphisms
\sigma_{\rvert A\times U}
and
q_{\rvert A\times U}
, where

\sigma,q\colon A\times\Hilb_{A}\longrightarrow\Hilb_{A}

are respectively the morphism defining the action of
A
and the second projection. Since each fiber of
\mathcal{X}_{U}\to U
is of general type, the subgroup
U
-scheme
G
is finite. It follows that the locus where
G
is trivial is an open subset
V\subset U
. Euler characteristics are locally constant whence (
1.2
) and (
1.1
) are satisfied exactly on a union of connected components
W\subset V\cap\Hilb_{A,L,P}
. Summing up, it follows that

\Hilb^{\star}_{A,L,P}\subset W

is the subset containing the image of the geometric points
\Spec\Omega\to W
such that the corresponding subvariety
X\subset A_{\Omega}
is not a product. To see that
\Hilb^{\star}_{A,L,P}
is constructible it suffices to show that its complement in
V
is constructible. By Noetherian induction we reduce to prove the following. Let
S\subset W
be an integral subvariety with generic point
\eta
and
\Omega
an algebraic closure of the residue field of
\eta
. Suppose that the geometric point
\Spec\Omega\to S
is such that the corresponding subvariety
X\subset A_{\Omega}
is a product. Then there is a non-empty open subset
S^{\prime}\subset S
such that for each geometric point
\bar{s}
of
S^{\prime}
the subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{s}}
is a product. By spreading out we may suppose that there are a quasi-finite étale morphism
\nu\colon\tilde{S}\to S
and subvarieties
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2}\subset A_{\tilde{S}}
such that the sum induces an isomorphism

\mathcal{X}_{1}\times_{\tilde{S}}\mathcal{X}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{X}\times_{\Hilb_{A}}\tilde{S}

of
\tilde{S}
-schemes. It follows the open subset
S^{\prime}=\nu(S)
does the job.
∎

2.2.
Proof of
C
in the nondivisible case

Let
K
be a number field and
\Sigma
a finite set of places of
K
. As in
section

1.6
we let
A
be an abelian scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
of relative dimension
g
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
g\geqslant 4
. Let
S
be an integral
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
-scheme of finite type with smooth generic fiber. Let
\mathcal{C}\subset A_{S}
be a smooth relative curve over
S
whose fiber
\mathcal{C}_{\bar{\eta}}
over a geometric generic point
\bar{\eta}
of
S
has ample normal bundle, is nondivisible and not constant up to translation. Then
S(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
is not Zariski dense in
S
.

Proof.

We may assume that
S(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
is nonempty, so that the generic fiber of
S
is geometrically connected. Clearly
\mathcal{C}_{\bar{\eta}}
is not a symmetric power of degree
\geqslant 2
of a curve, so the big monodromy criterion from
section

1.5
implies that
\mathcal{C}\to A_{S}
has big monodromy for most tuples of torsion characters. Now apply
D
.
∎

Theorem 2.3
.

Fix a polynomial
P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
of degree
d<(g-1)/2
and an ample line bundle
L
on
A_{K}
. Then up to translation by points in
A(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
there are only finitely many smooth geometrically connected nondivisible subvarieties
X\subset A_{K}
over
K
with ample normal bundle and embedded good reduction that are not a product, that have Hilbert polynomial
P
with respect to
L
, and that satisfy (
1.1
) and (
1.2
).

Proof.

Let
\Hilb_{A}
the Hilbert scheme of
A
and
H\subset\Hilb_{A}
the open subset where the universal family
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Hilb_{A}
is a smooth morphism. With notation as in
lemma

2.1
let

F\subset H(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})\cap\Hilb_{A_{K},P}^{\star}

be a subset and
S
an irreducible component of the Zariski closure of
F
in
H
. By Noetherian induction, it suffices to show that there are a nonempty open subset
S^{\prime}_{K}\subset S_{K}
, a morphism
a\colon S^{\prime}_{K}\to A_{K}
and a subvariety
X\subset A_{K}
such that

\mathcal{X}_{S^{\prime}_{K}}=X+a.

Let
\bar{\eta}
be a geometric generic point of
S
. By
[
JKLM23
, cor. 4.8]
the last property is equivalent to saying that
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}
is constant up to translation (in loc. cit. this is stated for families over an algebraically closed field, but the argument works in general). Let
S^{\prime}_{K}\subset S_{K}^{\textup{reg}}
be a nonempty open subset and
S^{\prime}\subset S
the maximal open subset with generic fiber
S^{\prime}_{K}
, that is,

S^{\prime}\;:=\;S\;\smallsetminus\;\textup{Zariski closure of $(S_{K}\smallsetminus S^{\prime}_{K})$ in $S$}.

By construction
S^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
is Zariski-dense in
S^{\prime}
. Arguing by contradiction, suppose
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}
is not constant up to a translation (hence
\dim S_{K}>0
). We are going to show that this implies that
S^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
is not Zariski dense yielding the desired contradiction.

Suppose that
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}
is not a symmetric power of a curve. Then the big monodromy criterion from
section

1.5
implies that the family
\mathcal{X}_{S^{\prime}}\to A_{S}^{\prime}
has big monodromy for most tuples of torsion characters. We conclude the proof by
D
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}
is a symmetric power of a curve. Up to enlarging
\Sigma
and shrinking
S^{\prime}_{K}
we may assume that there are a finite étale morphism
\nu\colon\tilde{S}\to S^{\prime}
and a smooth relative curve
\mathcal{C}\subset A_{\tilde{S}}
over
\tilde{S}
such that the sum morphism induces an isomorphism

\Sym^{d}_{\tilde{S}}\mathcal{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{S}}=\mathcal{X}_{S^{\prime}}\times_{S^{\prime}}\tilde{S}.

By the Chevalley-Weil theorem
[
CTZ22
, cor. 1.3]
, there is a finite extension
K^{\prime}
of
K
and a finite set of places
\Sigma^{\prime}
of
K^{\prime}
containing those above
\Sigma
such that the image of the induced map
\tilde{S}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}})\to S^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}})

contains
S^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
. The relative curve
\mathcal{C}
is not constant up to translation because otherwise
\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{S}}
would be so. Therefore
lemma

2.2
implies that the set
\tilde{S}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}})
is not Zariski dense, thus
S^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
is not Zariski dense either.
∎

2.3.
Proof of
C
in the general case

We go back to the notation of
C
thus
A
is an abelian variety of dimension
g
over
K
equipped with an ample line bundle
L
. Pick
P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
of degree
d<(g-1)/2
and consider the set
\mathcal{S}
of smooth geometrically connected subvarieties
X\subset A
over
K
that are not a product, that have

•

ample normal bundle,

•

Hilbert polynomial
P
with respect to
L
,

•

good reduction,

and that satisfy (
1.2
) and (
1.1
).

Let
H\subset\Hilb_{A}
be the locus of the Hilbert scheme of
A
over which the universal family
\mathcal{X}\to\Hilb_{A}
is smooth, with geometrically connected fibers and Hilbert polynomial
P
with respect to
L
. The function
H\to\mathbb{N}
,
h\mapsto\chi_{\textup{top}}(\mathcal{X}_{h})
is locally constant thus takes finitely many values because
H
is of finite type over
K
. For each
h\in H
the integer
\chi_{\textup{top}}(\mathcal{X}_{h})
is divisible by
|\Stab_{A}(\mathcal{X}_{h})|
hence the set

\mathcal{G}:=\{\Stab_{A}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\mid h\in H(K)\}

is finite. Up to enlarging
\Sigma
we may suppose that it contains all the places above the prime divisors of
\chi_{\textup{top}}(\mathcal{X}_{h})
for all
h\in H
. Then for
G\in\mathcal{G}
the abelian variety
A/G
has dimension
g
and embedded good reduction. Similarly for all
h\in H(K)
the subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{h}\subset A
has good reduction, thus so does

\mathcal{X}_{h}/\Stab_{A}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow A/\Stab_{A}(\mathcal{X}_{h}).

Since
\mathcal{G}
is finite it suffices to prove the statement for varieties
X\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\Stab_{A}(X)
equals one fixed
G\in\mathcal{G}
. In this case pick an ample line bundle
M
on the abelian variety
B:=A/G
. The line bundle
\pi^{\ast}M
on
A
is ample where
\pi\colon A\to B
is the projection. Moreover the function
H\to\mathbb{Q}[z]
associating to
h\in H
the Hilbert polynomial of
\mathcal{X}_{h}
with respect to
\pi^{\ast}M
takes finitely many values. Again by treating each polynomial separately we may suppose that all varieties
X\in\mathcal{S}
have the same Hilbert polynomial
Q
with respect to
\pi^{\ast}M
. Then the subvarieties
X/G\hookrightarrow B
are nondivisible and have Hilbert polynomial
Q/|G|
with respect to
M
. It follows from
theorem

2.3
applied to (the unique abelian scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
extending)
B
that the set

\{X/G\subset B\mid X\in\mathcal{S}\}/B(K)

is finite. The conclusion follows immediately because, by the Chevalley-Weil theorem, the isogeny
A\to B
induces a group morphism
A(K)\to B(K)
with finite cokernel.
∎

2.4.
Proof of
B
if there is a rational point

We now discuss the proof of
B
when the varieties in question admit a rational point. To do this, for very irregular varieties over a number field
K
we momentarily need a stronger version of good reduction outside a finite set of places
\Sigma
:

Definition 2.4
.

A very irregular variety
X
over
K
has
embedded good reduction
if there is a smooth projective scheme
\mathcal{X}
over
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
with generic fiber
X
such that, for any ring morphism
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}\to k
to an algebraically closed field
k
, the base change
\mathcal{X}_{k}
embeds into its Albanese variety.

When
X
has a
K
-rational point the defining property of
\mathcal{X}
is equivalent to the fact that the corresponding Albanese morphism
\mathcal{X}\to\Alb(\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
is a closed embedding. This relates to the notion of good reduction in
C
. With the notions, we prove the following:

Theorem 2.5
.

Fix
P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
of degree
d
. Then up to
K
-isomorphism there are only finitely many very irregular varieties
X
with embedded good reduction, a
K
-rational point, Hilbert polynomial
P
,
h^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\geqslant 2d+2
and (
1.1
), satisfying the numerical condition (
1.2
) above with
A=\Alb(X)
.

Proof.

Recall that by Matsusaka’s Big Theorem there is an integer
N\geqslant 1
depending only on
P
with the following property: For every smooth projective variety
X
over a field
k
, every ample line bundle
L
on
X
with Hilbert polynomial
P
and every
n\geqslant N
, the line bundle
L^{\otimes n}
is very ample and
\textup{H}^{i}(X,L^{\otimes n})
vanishes for all
i\geqslant 1
. We now proceed in the following steps:

Step 1.
In the first step we show that the varieties in question are parametrized by a
K
-scheme of finite type. Consider the functor

\mathcal{H}_{0}\colon(\textup{Sch}/K)\longrightarrow(\textup{Sets}),\hskip 10.00002ptS\longmapsto(f\colon X\to S,\;\varphi\colon\mathcal{O}_{S}^{P(N)}\to f_{\ast}\omega^{\otimes N}_{X/S},\;x\colon S\to X)

where

•

f
is a proper smooth morphism of relative dimension
d=\deg P
with geometrically connected fibers and relative canonical bundle
\omega_{X/S}=\det\Omega_{X/S}
,

•

\varphi
an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules,

•

x
is a section of
f\colon X\to S
,

such that the Hilbert polynomial of the fibers of
f
with respect to
\omega_{X/S}
is
P
and for each
s\in S
the line bundle
\omega_{X_{s}}^{\otimes N}
on
X_{s}
is very ample. Then
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is represented by a scheme
H_{0}
of finite type over
K
. Indeed the functor
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{0}
obtained from
\mathcal{H}_{0}
by dropping the datum of the section
x\colon S\to X
is represented by a quasi-projective variety
H^{\prime}_{0}
over
K
; see
[
Vie95
, th. 1.46]
. Then the functor
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is represented by the universal family
H_{0}
over
H_{0}^{\prime}
. Let

\mathcal{X}_{0}=H_{0}\times_{H^{\prime}_{0}}H_{0}\to H_{0}

be the universal family. With this notation the universal section
x_{0}\colon H_{0}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
is just the diagonal. The relative Picard scheme
\Pic^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{0}/H_{0})
is representable and we consider its dual abelian scheme
\Alb(\mathcal{X}_{0}/H_{0})
. The universal section
x_{0}
permits to define the Albanese morphism

a_{0}\colon\mathcal{X}_{0}\longrightarrow\Alb(X_{0}/H_{0}).

The locus
H\subset H_{0}
where
a_{0}
is a closed embedding with ample normal bundle is open. Let
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{0}\times_{H_{0}}H
be the universal family over
H
,
x\colon H\to\mathcal{X}
the universal section and
a\colon\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\Alb(\mathcal{X}/H)
the Albanese morphism.

Step 2.
In order to apply
C
we need a polarization on the Albanese and not only on the variety itself. Note that line bundles on an Albanese embedded variety do not necessarily extend to the whole Albanese. Nonetheless we can define a natural polarization on
\Alb(\mathcal{X}/H)
. Let
\mathcal{P}
be the Poincaré bundle
and
p
,
q
the first and second projection on
\mathcal{X}\times_{H}\Pic^{0}(\mathcal{X}/H)
. Since the line bundle
p^{\ast}\omega_{X/S}
is relatively ample with respect to
q
, there exists an integer
n\geqslant N
such that

\textup{R}^{i}q_{\ast}(p^{\ast}\omega^{\otimes n}_{\mathcal{X}/H}\otimes\mathcal{P})=0\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{for all}\hskip 10.00002pti\geqslant 1.

It follows that the coherent sheaf

\mathcal{F}=q_{\ast}(p^{\ast}\omega^{\otimes n}_{\mathcal{X}/H}\otimes\mathcal{P})

over
\Pic^{0}(\mathcal{X}/H)
is locally free and
[
Laz04
, th. 6.3.48]
implies that
\det\mathcal{F}^{\vee}

is a line bundle which is relatively ample with respect to
\Pic^{0}(\mathcal{X}/H)\to H
. We then consider its dual polarization
\mathcal{L}
on
\Alb(\mathcal{X}/H)
. The function
H\to\mathbb{Q}[z]
associating to
h\in H
the Hilbert polynomial of
\mathcal{X}_{h}
with respect to
\mathcal{L}
is locally constant. Since
H
has finitely many connected components, by treating each of them separately we may assume that
H
is
connected
. Therefore each fiber of
\mathcal{X}\to H
has Hilbert polynomial with respect to
\mathcal{L}
equal to some fixed
Q\in\mathbb{Q}[z]
.

Step 3.
In the third step we are going to reduce to some fixed ambient abelian variety. Let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of
h\in H(K)
for which
\mathcal{X}_{h}\hookrightarrow\Alb(\mathcal{X}_{h}/K)
has strong good reduction. As
H
is connected, the degree of the polarization
\mathcal{L}
on
\Alb(\mathcal{X}/H)
is constant and equal to
\rk\pi_{\ast}\omega_{\mathcal{X}/H}^{\otimes n}
where
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to H
denotes the structure morphism. Then Faltings’ theorem for polarized abelian varieties
[
Fal83
]
implies

|\{(\Alb(\mathcal{X}_{h}/K),\mathcal{L}_{h})\mid h\in\mathcal{S}\}/\cong|<\infty

where isomorphisms are understood to be over
K
. By treating each isomorphism class separately, we may assume

(\Alb(\mathcal{X}_{h}/K),\mathcal{L}_{h})\cong(A,L)\textup{ for all $h\in\mathcal{S}$}

for a fixed polarized abelian variety
(A,L)
over
K
. If we embed
\mathcal{X}_{h}
in
A
via such an isomorphism, then the Hilbert polynomial of
\mathcal{X}_{h}
with respect to
L
is
Q
and we conclude by applying
C
.
∎

2.5.
Models of canonically polarized varieties

In order to prove
B
in the general case, we need a few facts about models of very irregular varieties. As in the previous section, let
K
be a number field,
\Sigma
a finite set of places and
R=\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
. For a smooth scheme
\mathcal{X}
over
R
we denote by
\omega_{\mathcal{X}/R}=\det\Omega_{\mathcal{X}/R}
its relative canonical bundle. We will use the following key property:

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
X
be a very irregular variety with good reduction and
\mathcal{X}
a smooth proper
R
-scheme with generic fiber
X
such that, for any ring morphism
R\to k
to an algebraically closed field
k
, the base change
\mathcal{X}_{k}
embeds into its Albanese variety. Then, the relative canonical bundle
\omega_{\mathcal{X}/R}
is relatively ample.

Proof.

Relative ampleness can be tested after a finite faithfully flat base change, hence we may assume that
X
admits a
K
-rational point.
We show that for every point
s\in S=\Spec R
the canonical bundle
\omega_{s}=\omega_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}
of the fiber
\mathcal{X}_{s}
at
s
is ample. For the generic point
s=\eta
of
S
this is true by hypothesis because
X
is a very irregular variety. When
s
is a closed point, for any
n\geqslant 1
by semicontinuity we have

h^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{s},\omega_{s}^{\otimes n})\geqslant h^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta},\omega_{\eta}^{\otimes n})=h^{0}(X,\omega_{X}^{\otimes n}).

It follows that the line bundle
\omega_{s}
on
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is big, thus the variety
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is of general type. By
[
Abr94
, th. 3]
the stabilizer of the subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{s}\hookrightarrow\Alb(\mathcal{X}_{s})
is finite, and by
[
Abr94
, lemma 6]
the canonical bundle of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is ample.
∎

In the rest of this section, the only property of very irregular varieties that we are going to use is that they are
canonically polarized
, i.e. they are smooth projective varieties whose canonical bundle is ample.

Definition 2.7
.

A canonically polarized variety
X
over
K
has
good reduction
if there is a smooth proper
R
-scheme
\mathcal{X}
with generic fiber
X
and relatively ample relative canonical bundle
\omega_{\mathcal{X}/R}
.
We then call
\mathcal{X}
a
good model
for
X
.

Lemma

2.6
in particular implies that very irregular varieties with good reduction also have good reduction as canonically polarized varieties. As such, they have the following properties:

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
X
and
Y
be canonically polarized varieties over
K
with good reduction. Then, the following facts hold:

(1)

Given good models
\mathcal{X}
resp.
\mathcal{Y}
of
X
resp.
Y
, any isomorphism
X\to Y
of
K
-schemes extends uniquely to an isomorphism
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
of
R
-schemes.

(2)

Up to isomorphism of
R
-schemes, there is a unique good model of
X
.

(3)

Let
\bar{K}
be an algebraic closure of
K
. Then, up to
K
-isomorphism there are only finitely many canonically polarized varieties over
K
with good reduction that are isomorphic to
X
over
\bar{K}
.

Proof.

(1) follows from
[
MM64
, cor. 1]
because canonically polarized varieties are not ruled, (2) is a consequence of (1), and (3) is
[
Jav15
, lemma 4.1]
.
∎

2.6.
Proof of
B
in the general case

The proof of
B
in the general case goes by reduction to the case of varieties with rational points treated in
theorem

2.5
.

First, we construct a parameter space whose points with values in
R=\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
correspond to the varieties in the statement. To do this, as in the proof of
theorem

2.5
, fix an integer
N\geqslant 1
such that for every smooth projective variety
X
over a field
k
, every ample line bundle
L
on
X
with Hilbert polynomial
P
and every
n\geqslant N
, the line bundle
L^{\otimes n}
is very ample and
\textup{H}^{i}(X,L^{\otimes n})=0
for all
i\geqslant 1
. Up to enlarging
\Sigma
we may assume that the ring
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
is a principal ideal domain. Consider the functor

\mathcal{H}\colon(\textup{Sch}/R)\longrightarrow(\textup{Sets}),\hskip 10.00002ptS\longmapsto(f\colon X\to S,\;\varphi\colon\mathcal{O}_{S}^{P(N)}\to f_{\ast}\omega^{\otimes N}_{X/S})

where

•

f
is a proper smooth morphism of relative dimension
d=\deg P
with geometrically connected fibers and relative canonical bundle
\omega_{X/S}=\det\Omega_{X/S}
,

•

\varphi
an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules,

such that the Hilbert polynomial of the fibers of
f
with respect to
\omega_{X/S}
is
P
and for each
s\in S
the line bundle
\omega_{X_{s}}^{\otimes N}
on
X_{s}
is very ample. Then
\mathcal{H}
is represented by a scheme
H
of finite type over
R
; see
[
Vie95
, th. 1.46]
. Note that the varieties in the statement of
B
give rise to points in
H(R)
. Here we use that the global sections of the
N
-th power of the canonical bundle of the model form a
free

R
-module of rank
P(N)
because
R
is assumed to be a principal ideal domain.

We now reduce to the case where the universal family of varieties
\mathcal{X}\to H
admits a section. To do this, since the universal family is smooth over
H
, by
[
EGA IV4
, 17.16.3]
there is an étale surjective morphism
H^{\prime}\to H
and a section of

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{X}\times_{H}H^{\prime}\longrightarrow H^{\prime}.

By the Chevalley-Weil theorem
[
CTZ22
, cor. 1.3]
there are a finite extension of
K^{\prime}
of
K
and a finite set of places
\Sigma^{\prime}
of
K^{\prime}
including all those above
\Sigma
such that the image of the map
H^{\prime}(R^{\prime})\to H(R^{\prime})
contains
H(R)
where
R^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}_{K^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}}
. Thanks to
lemma

2.8
we reduce to proving that the varieties in the statement are only finitely many up to
K^{\prime}
-isomorphism. By base changing to
H^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime}
we may therefore assume that
\mathcal{X}\to H
has a section
x_{0}\colon H\to\mathcal{X}
.

Finally, we deal with the fact that the notion of good reduction in
theorem

2.5
is more restrictive than the one in
B
. For, consider the relative Albanese morphism
a_{0}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Pic^{0}(\mathcal{X}/H)
associated with the section
x_{0}
. Let
W\subset H
be the open subset flat over
R
where
a_{0}
is a closed embedding with ample normal bundle. Let
\bar{W}\subset H
be Zariski closure of
W_{K}
and let
W^{\prime}\subset\bar{W}
the biggest open subset having
W_{K}
as generic fiber, that is, the complement of the Zariski closure in
\bar{W}
of
\bar{W}_{K}\smallsetminus W_{K}
. We clearly have an inclusion
W\subset W^{\prime}
of open subsets of
H
and

W_{K}=W^{\prime}_{K}.

Since both
W
and
W^{\prime}
are of finite type over
R
we may assume
W=W^{\prime}
up to enlarging
\Sigma
. Now the varieties in the statement of statement of
B
correspond to
R
-points of
W^{\prime}
. But the equality
W=W^{\prime}
implies that these varieties have embedded good reduction in the sense of
definition

2.4
, hence we can conclude by applying
theorem

2.5
.
∎

2.7.
Proof of
A

For a smooth projective surface
X
the Riemann-Roch theorem together with Noether’s formula imply

\chi(X,\omega_{X}^{\otimes n})=\tfrac{1}{2}n(n-1)c_{1}(X)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{12}(c_{1}(X)^{2}+c_{2}(X)).

If
X
is of general type, then
c_{1}(X)^{2}\leqslant 3c_{2}(X)
by the Bogomolov-Miyaoka-Yau inequality. In particular once
c_{2}(X)
is fixed there are only finitely many possibilities for the Hilbert polynomial of
X
with respect to the canonical bundle. We then conclude by
B
. ∎

3.
Buildings and filtrations on reductive groups

In this section we gather some generalities about reductive groups that will be used throughout the rest of the paper. Let
k
be a perfect field.

3.1.
Reductive groups

For an affine algebraic group
G
over
k
let
\rad G
be the unipotent radical of
G^{\circ}
. In this paper
G
is
reductive
if
\rad G
is trivial. Note that
G
is not necessarily connected. When
k
is of characteristic
0
, an affine algebraic group over
k
is reductive if and only its category of representations is semisimple. Let
G
be a reductive group over
k
. Given a cocharacter
\lambda\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\to G
consider the algebraic subgroup
P_{\lambda}\subset G
whose points with values in a
k
-scheme
S
are those
g\in G(S)
such that the limit

\lim_{t\to 0}\lambda(t)g\lambda(t)^{-1}

exists, that is, the morphism
\lambda g\lambda^{-1}\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\times S\to G
extends to
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times S
. The connected component of
P_{\lambda}
is a parabolic subgroup of
G^{\circ}
. Consider the subgroup
U_{\lambda}
of
P_{\lambda}
made of those
g
for which
\lim_{t\to 0}\lambda(t)g\lambda(t)^{-1}
is the neutral element of
G
. The subgroup
U_{\lambda}
is connected by definition and coincides with the unipotent radical
\rad P_{\lambda}
of
P_{\lambda}
. If
L_{\lambda}
is the centralizer of the image of
\lambda
in
G
, then the projection onto
P_{\lambda}/\rad P_{\lambda}
induces an isomorphism

L_{\lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}P_{\lambda}/\rad P_{\lambda}.

See
[
Mar03
, prop. 5.2]
for these facts. When
G
is connected, parabolic subgroups of
G
are parametrized by a proper smooth
k
-scheme
\Par(G)
. Its Stein factorization
\Type(G)=\Spec\Gamma(\Par(G),\mathcal{O}_{\Par(G)})
is finite étale over
k
and the preimage
\Par_{t}(G)
of
t\in\Type(G)(k)
via the natural map
\Par(G)\to\Type(G)
is a geometrically connected component. A parabolic subgroup
P\subset G
is said of type
t(P)=t
if the point
[P]\in\Par(G)(k)
defined by
P
lies in
\Par_{t}(G)
.

3.2.
The rational building

We recall the notion of the rational (or vectorial) building of a reductive group
G
over
k
; when
G
is connected this can be found in
[
Rou81
, §2]
. Write
X_{\ast}(G)
for the set of cocharacters of
G
.

Definition 3.1
.

The
rational building

\mathcal{B}(G,k)
of
G
is the quotient

\mathcal{B}(G,k)\;:=\;(X_{\ast}(G)\times(\mathbb{N}\smallsetminus\{0\}))/\sim

where
(\lambda,n)\sim(\lambda^{\prime},n^{\prime})
if there is
g\in P_{\lambda}(k)
such that
\lambda^{\prime n}=g\lambda^{n^{\prime}}g^{-1}
.

The equivalence class of
(\lambda,n)
is written
[\tfrac{\lambda}{n}]
.
For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
with
b\geqslant 1
and
x=[\tfrac{\lambda}{n}]
write
\tfrac{a}{b}x=[\tfrac{\lambda^{a}}{bn}]
. The group
G(k)
acts on the rational building by conjugating cocharacters. The stabilizer of a point
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is
P_{x}(k)
where
P_{x}:=P_{\lambda}
and
x=[\tfrac{\lambda}{n}]
. We write
t(x)
for the type of the parabolic subgroup
P_{x}^{\circ}\subset G^{\circ}
. The construction of the rational building is functorial: for a morphism of algebraic groups
f\colon G\to G^{\prime}
with
G^{\prime}
reductive, composing with
f
gives rise to map

f_{\ast}\colon\mathcal{B}(G,k)\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}(G^{\prime},k).

Lemma 3.2
.

The natural map
i\colon\mathcal{B}(G^{\circ},k)\to\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is bijective.

Proof.

Since any cocharacter takes values in
G^{\circ}
the map
i
is clearly surjective. To see that
i
is injective let
(\lambda,n)
and
(\lambda^{\prime},n^{\prime})\in X_{\ast}(G)
be couples made of a cocharater and a positive integer such that there is
g\in P_{\lambda}(k)
with
\lambda^{\prime n}=g\lambda^{n^{\prime}}g^{-1}
. We have to prove that we can choose such an element
g
in the connected component of
P_{\lambda}
. The image
\bar{g}
of
g
in
L_{\lambda}\cong P_{\lambda}/\rad P_{\lambda}
commutes with
\lambda
thus

\lambda^{\prime n}=g\lambda^{n^{\prime}}g^{-1}=g\bar{g}^{-1}\lambda^{n^{\prime}}\bar{g}g^{-1}.

This concludes the proof because
g\bar{g}^{-1}\in\rad P_{\lambda}\subset P_{\lambda}^{\circ}
.
∎

The map
f_{\ast}
is injective resp. surjective if and only if
f
has finite kernel resp. is surjective. The
apartment
associated with a maximal split torus
T\subset G
is the subset
\mathcal{B}(T,k)=X_{\ast}(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
.

Example 3.3
.

Suppose
G=\GL(V)
for a finite dimensional
k
-vector space
V
. For a point
x=[\tfrac{\lambda}{n}]\in\mathcal{B}(\GL(V),k)
let
V=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}

be the decomposition of
V
in isotypical factors under the action of
\lambda
; that is
V_{i}
is the subspace where
\lambda(t)
acts as
v\mapsto t^{i}v
. For
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
set

\textup{F}^{\alpha}V=\bigoplus_{i\geqslant\alpha n}V_{i}.

This defines a separated exhaustive nonincreasing filtration
F^{\bullet}V
on
V
indexed by rational numbers. This procedure sets up a
\GL(V)(k)
-equivariant bijection

\mathcal{B}(\GL(V),k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{separated exhaustive nonincreasing filtrations }\\
\textup{of $V$ indexed by rational numbers}\end{array}\right\}.

Let
G=\GO(V,\theta)
resp.
G=\GSp(V,\theta)
for a nondegenerate pairing
\theta
on
V
which is symmetric resp. alternating. Then
\mathcal{B}(G,k)\subset\mathcal{B}(\GL(V),k)
is the subset of
self-dual
filtrations, i.e. such that there is
c\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
(F^{\alpha}V)^{\bot}=F^{c-\alpha}V
for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Example 3.4
.

(1) For reductive groups
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
over
k
we have

\mathcal{B}(G_{1}\times G_{2},k)=\mathcal{B}(G_{1},k)\times\mathcal{B}(G_{2},k).

(2) Given a finite extension
k^{\prime}
of
k
and
G^{\prime}
a reductive group over
k^{\prime}
we have

\mathcal{B}(G,k)=\mathcal{B}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime})

where
G:=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}G^{\prime}
is the Weil restriction.

3.3.
Filtrations

We will need a similar description for points in a general reductive group in terms of filtrations. In order to do this let
G
be an affine algebraic group over
k
. Recall that a representation of
G
is a
k
-vector space
V
together with the structure of a comodule over the Hopf algebra
k[G]:=\Gamma(G,\mathcal{O}_{G})
. Such a structure is given by a map
\sigma\colon V\to V\otimes k[G]
called
comultiplication
henceforth.
When
V
is finite dimensional this is equivalent to the datum of a morphism of algebraic groups
G\to\GL(V)
. Let
\textup{Rep}(G)
be the category of finite dimensional representations. A
filtration functor
on
\textup{Rep}(G)
is a collection
F^{\bullet}=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}}
of exact functors
F^{\alpha}\colon\textup{Rep}(G)\to\Vect_{k}
such that

(F1)

for each
V\in\textup{Rep}(G)
the collection
F^{\bullet}V=(F^{\alpha}V)_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}}
is a non-increasing, exhausting and separated filtration on
V
;

(F2)

the associated graded functor
\gr^{\bullet}
is exact;

(F3)

for
V,W\in\textup{Rep}(G)
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
,

F^{\alpha}(V\otimes W)=\sum_{\alpha=\beta+\gamma}F^{\beta}V\otimes F^{\gamma}W.

Note that in fact there is an integer
n\geqslant 1
for which
\gr^{\alpha}=0
if
\alpha n\not\in\mathbb{Z}
: this follows from the existence of a tensor generator for
\textup{Rep}(G)
, see
[
DMOS82
, Tannakian categories, prop. 2.20 (b)]
. This is what is called an
exact
\otimes
-filtration
in
[
SR72
, IV.2.1]
except for the fact that filtrations are only indexed by integers in there. For instance a cocharacter
\lambda\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\to G
determines a filtration functor by
example

3.3
. Conversely, every filtration functor with
\gr^{\alpha}=0
if
\alpha\not\in\mathbb{Z}
arise in this way when
\characteristic(k)=0

[
SR72
, prop. IV.2.2.2]
. The datum of a filtration functor is encoded by a suitable filtration induced on
k[G]
. More precisely, let

\mu\colon k[G]\to k[G]\otimes k[G]
be the comultiplication of the Hopf algebra
k[G]
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu\colon k[G]\otimes k[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k[G]\otimes k[G])\otimes(k[G]\otimes k[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[G]\otimes k[G]\otimes k[G],}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\otimes\mu}

where the last arrow is
a\otimes f\otimes b\otimes g\mapsto a\otimes b\otimes fg
.

Definition 3.5
.

A filtration
k[G]^{\bullet}=(k[G]^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}}
on
k[G]
is
Hopf
if it is nonincreasing, exhausting, separated and for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
we have:

(HF1)

\displaystyle k[G]^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\;=\;\mu^{-1}(k[G]\otimes k[G]^{\alpha}),

(HF2)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=\beta+\gamma}k[G]^{\beta}\otimes k[G]^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\;=\;\nu^{-1}(k[G]\otimes k[G]\otimes k[G]^{\alpha}).

Let
X=\Spec A
be a variety over
k
with a
right
action of
G
. The action is defined by an
k
-algebra morphism

\sigma\colon A\to A\otimes k[G]
. With
\sigma
as comultiplication
A
is a representation of
G
and as such is an increasing union of finite dimensional representations. Given a filtration functor
F^{\bullet}
on
\textup{Rep}(G)
, the
k
-algebra
A
inherits a filtration
F^{\bullet}A
. Let
G
act on itself by right multiplication; the arising representation on
k[G]
is called the
regular representation
. The preceding discussion with
A=k[G]
yields a filtration
F^{\bullet}k[G]
on
k[G]
which is Hopf. Indeed (
HF1
) holds because the group law
G\times G\to G
is equivariant with respect the right action
(x,y)g=(x,yg)
of
G
on
G\times G
. On other hand (
HF2
) holds because the filtration functor
F^{\bullet}
is compatible with tensor product.

Conversely, let
k[G]^{\bullet}
be a Hopf filtration on
k[G]
and
V
a representation with comultiplication
\sigma\colon V\to V\otimes k[G]
. For
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
set

F^{\alpha}V:=\sigma^{-1}(V\otimes k[G]^{\alpha}).

Note that the construction of the filtration
F^{\bullet}V=(F^{\alpha}V)_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}}
is functorial with respect to
G
-equivariant
k
-linear maps.

Proposition 3.6
.

The collection of functors
F^{\alpha}
is a filtration functor on
\textup{Rep}(G)
. Moreover, the above construction are mutually inverse and set up a bijection

\{\textup{filtration functors on $\textup{Rep}(G)$}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\{\textup{Hopf filtrations on $k[G]$}\}.

Proof.

For a morphism
\varphi\colon V\to W
of representations we have, for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
,

F^{\alpha}V=\varphi^{-1}(F^{\alpha}W)\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{if $\varphi$ is injective},\hskip 20.00003ptF^{\alpha}W=\varphi(F^{\alpha}V)\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{if $\varphi$ is surjective}.

It follows that the functors
F^{\alpha}\colon\textup{Rep}(G)\to\Vect_{k}
are exact and that the collection of functors
F^{\bullet}=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}}
satisfies (F1) and (F2). To prove (F3) consider representations
V
and
W
with comultiplications
\sigma_{V}\colon V\to V\otimes k[G]
and
\sigma_{W}\colon W\to W\otimes k[G]
respectively. The comultiplication on
V\otimes W
is then defined by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{V\otimes W}\colon V\otimes W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes k[G]\otimes W\otimes k[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes W\otimes k[G]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{V}\otimes\sigma_{W}}

where the latter arrow is
v\otimes f\otimes w\otimes g\mapsto v\otimes w\otimes fg
. Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes W\otimes k[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V\otimes k[G])\otimes(W\otimes k[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V\otimes k[G])\otimes(W\otimes k[G])\otimes k[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes W\otimes k[G]\otimes k[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes W\otimes k[G]\otimes k[G]\otimes k[G]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{V\otimes W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{V}\otimes\sigma_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{V}\otimes\sigma_{W}\otimes\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\otimes\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{V\otimes W}\otimes\sigma_{k[G]\otimes k[G]}}

where
s(v\otimes f\otimes w\otimes g)=v\otimes w\otimes f\otimes g
. Then by (
HF2
) we have:

(V\otimes W)^{\alpha}=\sum_{\alpha=\beta+\gamma}V^{\beta}\otimes W^{\gamma}.

Therefore
F^{\bullet}
is a filtration functor. Property (
HF1
) implies
F^{\alpha}k[G]=k[G]^{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
, thus the map
H
is surjective. In order to prove that
H
is injective, let now
F^{\bullet}
be a filtration functor on
\textup{Rep}(G)
. Given a representation
V
the comultiplication
V\to V\otimes k[G]
is
G
-equivariant with
G
acting on
V\otimes k[G]
trivially on
V
and by right-multiplication on
k[G]
. Since the comultiplication is injective, the exactness of
F^{\bullet}
implies
F^{\alpha}V=\sigma^{-1}(V\otimes F^{\alpha}k[G])
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
and all finite dimensional representations
V
. This shows that
H
is injective and concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
f\colon G\to H
be a morphism of affine algebraic groups over
k
. Then the natural map

\{\textup{filtration functors on $\textup{Rep}(G)$}\}\;\longrightarrow\;\{\textup{filtration functors on $\textup{Rep}(H)$}\}

is injective if
f
is injective, and is bijective if the morphism
G^{\circ}\to H^{\circ}
induced by
f
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Suppose
f
injective. Then
f
is a closed immersion and the induced morphism of
k
-algebras
\varphi\colon k[H]\to k[G]
is surjective. By
proposition

3.6
it suffices to show that the corresponding map

\{\textup{Hopf functors on $\textup{Rep}(G)$}\}\;\longrightarrow\;\{\textup{Hopf functors on $\textup{Rep}(H)$}\}

is injective. The above map associates to a Hopf filtration
k[G]^{\bullet}
on
k[G]
, the filtration
k[H]^{\bullet}
on
k[H]
induced by the action of right multiplication of
G
on
H
. Now since
f
is
G
-equivariant with respect to right multiplication of
G
, we deduce that
k[G]^{\bullet}
is the image of
k[H]^{\bullet}
via
\varphi
. Thus
k[G]^{\bullet}
can be recovered
k[H]^{\bullet}
, showing that desired injectivity.

For the second statement, it suffices to show that the natural map

\{\textup{filtration functors on $\textup{Rep}(G^{\circ})$}\}\;\longrightarrow\;\{\textup{filtration functors on $\textup{Rep}(G)$}\}

is surjective, as we already know that it is injective. Given a filtration functor
F^{\bullet}
on
\textup{Rep}(G)
up to rescaling we may assume that
\gr^{\alpha}=0
if
\alpha\not\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
F^{\bullet}
comes from a cocharacter. But a cocharacter has necessarily values in
G^{\circ}
as
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is connected. Thus
F^{\bullet}
comes from a filtration functor on
G^{\circ}
.
∎

Given a filtration functor
F^{\bullet}
consider the algebraic subgroup
P\subset G
whose points with values in a
k
-scheme
S
are those
g\in G(S)
such that

g(F^{\alpha}V\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{S})=F^{\alpha}V\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{S}\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{for all $\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}$}.

When
G
is a reductive group, the subgroup
P
is parabolic
[
SR72
, Prop. IV.2.2.5]
and, if
F^{\bullet}
is given by a cocharacter
\lambda
, then
P
is the subgroup
P_{\lambda}
defined in the previous section. The filtration functors associated to cocharacters
\lambda
,
\lambda^{\prime}
then coincide if and only if
\lambda^{\prime}
is conjugate to
\lambda
under
P_{\lambda}
. Summing up:

Corollary 3.8
.

Suppose
\characteristic(k)=0
and
G
reductive. Then the above constructions set up bijections

\mathcal{B}(G,k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\{\textup{filtration functors on $\textup{Rep}(G)$}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\{\textup{Hopf filtrations on $k[G]$}\}.

3.4.
Projection onto Levi quotients

Let
G
be a reductive group and
Q\subset G^{\circ}
a parabolic subgroup of its connected component. For any
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
there is a cocharacter
\lambda
with values in
Q
such that
x=[\tfrac{\lambda}{n}]
for some
n\geqslant 1
: Indeed
P^{\circ}_{x}\cap Q
contains a maximal split torus, and by
[
BT65
, prop. 11.6]
all maximal split tori are conjugate in
P_{x}^{\circ}
. Let

\pi_{Q}\colon\hskip 10.00002ptQ\;\longrightarrow\;Q^{\textup{ss}}\;:=\;Q/\rad Q

be the projection onto the Levi quotient. Then the point

\pr_{Q}(x):=[\tfrac{\pi_{Q}\circ\lambda}{n}]\in\mathcal{B}(Q^{\textup{ss}},k)

does not depend on the chosen
\lambda
. The so-defined map
\pr_{Q}\colon\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to\mathcal{B}(Q^{\textup{ss}},k)
is
Q(k)
-equivariant and surjective.

Example 3.9
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional
k
-vector space and
G=\GL(V)
. The parabolic subgroup
Q
then is the stabilizer of a flag

W_{0}=0\subset W_{1}\subset\cdots\subset W_{n}\subset W_{n+1}=V.

The reductive quotient
Q^{\textup{ss}}
is identified with
\prod_{i=0}^{n}\GL(\bar{W}_{i})
where
\bar{W}_{i}=W_{i+1}/W_{i}
. If
F^{\bullet}V
is the filtration of
V
induced by
x
, then the point
\pr_{Q}(x)
can be seen as the tuple of filtrations
(F^{\bullet}\bar{W}_{i})_{i}
where

F^{\alpha}\bar{W}_{i}=F^{\alpha}V\cap W_{i+1}\;/\;F^{\alpha}V\cap W_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}$}.

Remark 3.10
.

In general seeing points of the building as Hopf filtrations, the Hopf filtration on
k[Q^{\textup{ss}}]
corresponding to
\pr_{Q}(x)
is obtained as follows: First take the image under the surjection
k[G]\to k[Q]
of the filtration on
k[G]
given by
x
; then intersect the resulting filtration with
k[Q^{\textup{ss}}]\subset k[Q]
.

Fibers of
\pr_{Q}
are described in the following:

Lemma 3.11
.

For
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
the map
\rad Q(k)\to\mathcal{B}(G,k)
,
g\mapsto gx
has values in
\pr_{Q}^{-1}(\pr_{Q}(x))
and induces a bijection

f\colon(\rad Q/\rad Q\cap P_{x})(k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pr_{Q}^{-1}(\pr_{Q}(x)).

In particular we have
\Stab_{Q^{\textup{ss}}}(\pr_{Q}(x))=(Q\cap P_{x})/(\rad Q\cap P_{x})
.

Proof.

First of all notice that the group
\rad Q\cap P_{x}
is unipotent thus the vanishing of the first Galois cohomology group yields

(\rad Q/\rad Q\cap P_{x})(k)=\rad Q(k)/(\rad Q(k)\cap P_{x}(k)).

The
Q(k)
-equivariance of
\pr_{Q}
implies that
f
has values in
\pr_{Q}^{-1}(\pr_{Q}(x))
and is injective. To prove that
f
is surjective let
x^{\prime}\in\pr_{Q}^{-1}(\pr_{Q}(x))
and
L,L^{\prime}\subset Q
Levi factors such that
\mathcal{B}(L,k),\mathcal{B}(L^{\prime},k)\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
contain respectively
x
and
x^{\prime}
. Since all Levi factors of
Q
are conjugated under
\rad Q
there is
u\in\rad Q(k)
such that
L=uL^{\prime}u^{-1}
. Therefore
ux^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{B}(L,k)
and

\pr_{Q}(ux^{\prime})=\pr_{Q}(x^{\prime})=\pr_{Q}(x).

The projection
\pi_{Q}\colon Q\to Q^{\textup{ss}}
induces an isomorphism
L\cong Q^{\textup{ss}}
thus the composite map

\mathcal{B}(L,k)\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}(G,k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pr_{Q}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{B}(Q^{\textup{ss}},k)

is a bijection. Therefore
x=ux^{\prime}
as desired.
∎

3.5.
Positivity

Let
V
be a finite dimensional
k
-vector space,
y\in\mathcal{B}(\GL(V),k)
and
F^{\bullet}V
the filtration on
V
associated to
y
as in
example

3.3
. The
weight
of
y
(or equivalently of the filtration
F^{\bullet}V
) is the rational number

\wt(y):=\frac{1}{\dim V}\langle x,\det\rangle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}}\alpha\frac{\dim\gr^{\alpha}V}{\dim V}.

Let
Q\subset G^{\circ}
be a parabolic subgroup. The adjoint representation of
Q
restricts to a representation
\rho\colon Q\to\GL(\Lie\rad Q)
because the unipotent radical is a normal subgroup. The determinant of
\rho
is trivial on
\rad Q
thus it defines a character

\delta_{Q}\colon Q^{\textup{ss}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}

called the
modular character
henceforth.
2
2

2

This differs from
[
LS20
]
where the modular character is the inverse of the above.
Let
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
and let
L\subset G
be a Levi factor such that
x\in\mathcal{B}(L,k)
. Unwinding the definition we find that the weight of the point
\rho_{\ast}x\in\mathcal{B}(\GL(\Lie\rad Q),k)
is

\wt(\rho_{\ast}x)=\langle\pr_{Q}(x),\delta_{Q}\rangle,

where
V=\Lie\rad Q
. Note that the right-hand side of the previous identity does not depend on the chosen
L
.

Definition 3.12
.

The point
x
is
positive with respect to
Q
if
\langle\pr_{Q}(x),\delta_{Q}\rangle\geqslant 0
. In this case we write
(x,Q)\succeq 0
. We will as well say that
x
is
Q
-positive or that the couple
(x,Q)
is positive.

Example 3.13
.

Let
G=\GL(V)
for some finite dimensional
k
-vector space
V
and let
Q\subset G
be the parabolic subgroup stabilizing a flag

0=W_{0}\subset W_{1}\subset\cdots\subset W_{r}\subset W_{r+1}=V

Then
Q^{\textup{ss}}=\prod_{i=0}^{r}\GL(\bar{W}_{i})
where
\bar{W}_{i}=W_{i+1}/W_{i}
. Let
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
, and let
F^{\bullet}V
be the associated filtration. Since the building is compatible with products by
example

3.4
, we have

\pr_{Q}(x)\;=\;(\bar{x}_{0},\dots,\bar{x}_{r})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\bar{x}_{i}\;\in\;\mathcal{B}(\GL(\bar{W}_{i}),k).

The filtration on
\bar{W}_{i}
defined by
\bar{x}_{i}
is given by

F^{\alpha}\bar{W}_{i}\;=\;(W_{i+1}\cap F^{\alpha}V)/(W_{i}\cap F^{\alpha}V)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for}\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}.

With this notation the point
x
is
Q
-positive if and only if

\sum_{i<j}\dim\bar{W}_{i}\dim\bar{W}_{j}(\wt(F^{\bullet}\bar{W}_{i})-\wt(F^{\bullet}\bar{W}_{j}))\geqslant 0.

Indeed, as a vector space

\Lie\rad Q\;=\;\bigoplus_{i<j}\Hom(\bar{W}_{j},\bar{W}_{i}),

and hence

\det(\Lie\rad Q)\;=\;\bigotimes_{i<j}\left(\det(\bar{W}_{j}^{\vee})\right)^{\otimes\dim\bar{W}_{i}}\otimes\left(\det(\bar{W}_{i})\right)^{\otimes\dim\bar{W}_{j}}.

If we denote by
\delta_{i}\colon Q^{\textup{ss}}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
the character defined by the action on
\det(\bar{W}_{i})
, it follows that

\delta_{Q}\;=\;\sum_{i<j}\left(\dim\bar{W}_{j}\cdot\delta_{i}-\dim\bar{W}_{i}\cdot\delta_{j}\right)

where we use additive notation for characters in the group
\Hom(Q^{\textup{ss}},\mathbb{G}_{m})
. Here we have
\langle\pr_{Q}(x),\delta_{i}\rangle=\dim\bar{W}_{i}\cdot\wt(F^{\bullet}\bar{W}_{i})
by the definition of the weight of a filtration, hence

\langle\pr_{Q}(x),\delta_{Q}\rangle\;=\;\sum_{i<j}\dim\bar{W}_{i}\dim\bar{W}_{j}\left(\wt(F^{\bullet}\bar{W}_{i})-\wt(F^{\bullet}\bar{W}_{j})\right)

and the claimed characterisation of
Q
-positivity follows.

Example 3.14
.

If
G
is the product of reductive groups
G_{i}
for
i\in I
, then
x=(x_{i})_{i\in I}
with
x_{i}\in\mathcal{B}(G_{i},k)
and
Q=\prod_{i\in I}Q_{i}
with parabolic subgroups
Q_{i}\subset G_{i}^{\circ}
. In this case

\langle\pr_{Q}(x),\delta_{Q}\rangle\;=\;\sum_{i\in I}\langle\pr_{Q_{i}}(x),\delta_{Q_{i}}\rangle.

In particular, if
x
is
Q
-positive, then
x_{i}
is
Q_{i}
-positive for at least one
i\in I
.

Example 3.15
.

If
G=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}G^{\prime}
is the Weil restriction of a reductive group
G^{\prime}
over a finite extension
k^{\prime}
of
k
, then we have
Q=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}Q^{\prime}
for some parabolic subgroup
Q^{\prime}\subset G^{\prime\circ}
. The identification
\mathcal{B}(G,k)=\mathcal{B}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime})
then leads to

\langle\pr_{Q}(x),\delta_{Q}\rangle\;=\;[k^{\prime}:k]\,\langle\pr_{Q^{\prime}}(x),\delta_{Q^{\prime}}\rangle.

In particular we see that
x
is
Q
-positive if and only if it is
Q^{\prime}
-positive.

3.6.
Full subgroups

Let
V
be a finite dimensional
k
-vector space and
G\subset\GL(V)
a reductive subgroup. Any point
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
defines a filtration on
V
indexed by rational numbers; we denote the filtration steps by
V_{x}^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
and the associated graded by
