for all
g\in\GL(V)(k)
and

\upsilon_{-x}(t)=\upsilon_{x}(d-t)-d\wt(x).

Definition 6.1
.

Let
G
be a reductive group over
k
and
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. The
adjoint polygon
is the function
\Upsilon_{G,x}:=\upsilon_{\textup{ad}_{\ast}x}\colon[0,\dim G]\to\mathbb{R}

where
\textup{ad}\colon G\to\GL(\Lie G)
is the adjoint representation. If the group is clear from the context we will omit it.

The adjoint polygon
\Upsilon_{G,x}
is concave and satisfies
\Upsilon_{G,cx}=c\Upsilon_{G,x}
for all rational numbers
c\geqslant 0
,
\Upsilon_{G,gx}=\Upsilon_{G,x}
for all
g\in G(k)
,
\Upsilon_{G,x}=\Upsilon_{G^{\circ},x}
and

\Upsilon_{G,x}(t)=\Upsilon_{G,x}(d-t),

thus
\Upsilon_{G,x}(\dim G)=0
. The above identity follows from
\textup{ad}_{\ast}(-x)=\textup{ad}_{\ast}x
. In (
6.3
) we will prove an explicit formula of
\Upsilon_{G,x}
in terms of roots.

6.2.
The main dimension estimate

Let
G
be reductive group and
\alpha\in\Aut(G)
an automorphism of
G
as an algebraic group. In practice
\alpha
will be the conjugation by a Frobenius operator obtained by comparison between crystalline and de Rham cohomology. To state the main result we need some more notation.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
and
Q,Q^{\prime}\subset G^{\circ}
be parabolic subgroups. The couples
(x,Q)
and
(x^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
are
\alpha
-equivalent
if there is a point
g\in G(k)
such that

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}=gQg^{-1},

\displaystyle g\alpha(g)^{-1}\in\rad Q^{\prime},

\displaystyle\pr_{Q^{\prime}}(gx)=\pr_{Q^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}),

where
\pr_{Q^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to\mathcal{B}(Q^{\textup{ss}},k)
is the projection defined in
section

3.4
. If this is the case we write
(x,Q)\sim_{\alpha}(x^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
.

If
(x,Q)\sim_{\alpha}(x^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, then
\alpha(Q)=Q
if and only if
\alpha(Q^{\prime})=Q^{\prime}
. In our application the above equivalence relation will arise by taking semisimplification of global Galois representations and then applying
p
-adic Hodge theory in a prime of good reduction, see
proposition

4.12
. Since we will not be able to have big monodromy for a tuple of characters that form a full local Galois orbit, we will have to project onto certain direct factors. To account for this, consider an epimorphism
f\colon G\to\bar{G}
of reductive groups. Let

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Par(G^{\circ})&\longrightarrow&\Par(\bar{G}^{\circ}),\\[3.00003pt]
P&\longmapsto&\bar{P}:=f(P)\end{array}\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{B}(G,k)&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{B}(\bar{G},k),\\[3.00003pt]
x&\longmapsto&\bar{x}:=f_{\ast}x\end{array}

be the induced maps. Note that with the above notation we have
\bar{P}_{x}=P_{\bar{x}}
.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
and
Q\subset G^{\circ}
be a parabolic subgroup stable under
\alpha
. Consider

[x,Q]_{\alpha}\;:=\;\left\{(P^{\circ}_{x^{\prime}},Q^{\prime})\mid(x^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\sim_{\alpha}(x,Q)\textup{ and }(\bar{x}^{\prime},\bar{Q}^{\prime})\succeq 0\right\}\;\subset\;\Par(G^{\circ})\times\Par(G^{\circ}),

where
(\bar{x}^{\prime},\bar{Q}^{\prime})\succeq 0
means that
\bar{x}^{\prime}
is
\bar{Q}^{\prime}
-positive in the sense of
definition

3.12
. Note that the notion of
\alpha
-equivalence depends on the group
G
and not only on its connected component. We will also write
[x,Q]_{G,\alpha}:=[x,Q]_{\alpha}
to emphasize this.

Let
\mathfrak{H}\subset\Par_{t(x)}(G^{\circ})
be a smooth subvariety such that the composite map

\mathfrak{H}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\Par_{t(x)}(G^{\circ})\longrightarrow\hskip-14.0pt\longrightarrow\Par_{t(\bar{x})}(\bar{G}^{\circ})

is a smooth proper morphism with equidimensional fibers. The main result of this section is an upper bound for the codimension of the Zariski closure of
p([x,Q]_{\alpha})\cap\mathfrak{H}
in
\mathfrak{H}
where

p\colon\Par(G^{\circ})\times\Par(G^{\circ})\longrightarrow\Par(G^{\circ}),\hskip 10.00002pt(P,Q)\longmapsto P,

is the first projection. Note that
[x,Q]_{\alpha}
is stable under the action of the elements of
G(k)
fixed under
\alpha
so the dimension of the latter will naturally play a role. In fact we obtain a bound in terms of the
semisimple part
\alpha^{\textup{ss}}
in the Jordan-Chevalley decomposition of
\alpha

seen as an element of the algebraic group
\Aut(G)
. Let

C:=C_{G}(\alpha^{\textup{ss}})=\{g\in G:\alpha^{\textup{ss}}(g)=g\}\subset G.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
and
Q\subset G^{\circ}
a parabolic subgroup with
\alpha(Q)=Q
and
(\bar{x},\bar{Q})\succeq 0
. Let
n\geqslant 1
be an integer such that

(6.1)

\displaystyle n\leqslant\dim\Par_{t(\bar{x})}(\bar{G}^{\circ}),

(6.2)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\bar{G},\bar{x}}(n-1)+\Upsilon_{\bar{G},\bar{x}}(n-1+\tfrac{1}{2}(\dim\bar{P}_{x}^{\textup{ss}}-\rk\bar{G}))<\max\Upsilon_{\bar{G},\bar{x}}.

Then the Zariski closure of
p([x,Q]_{\alpha})\cap\mathfrak{H}
in
\mathfrak{H}
has codimension
\geqslant n-\dim C
.

As they stand inequalities (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) are in general hard to verify. In practice we will apply the theorem in a situation where the quotient
\bar{G}
decomposes as a high enough power of some given reductive group
H
, in which case
theorem

6.4
leads to the following bound:

Corollary 6.5
.

Fix a finite subset
I\subset\mathbb{Q}
and integers
n,d\geqslant 1
. Then there exists in integer
N_{0}=N_{0}(I,n,d)\geqslant 1
independent of
k
with the following property: Suppose we are given

•

a reductive group
H
of dimension
d
,

•

a point
y\in\mathcal{B}(H,k)
such that all slopes of the adjoint polygon

\Upsilon_{H,y}
lie in
I
and

2\dim\Stab_{H}(y)^{\textup{ss}}<\dim H+\rk H,

•

a reductive group
G
over
k
and
\alpha\in\Aut(G)
with

\dim C_{G}(\alpha^{\textup{ss}})\leqslant\dim H,

•

a surjective morphism
f\colon G\to\bar{G}:=H^{N}
for some integer
N\geqslant N_{0}
,

•

a point
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
with
f_{*}(x)=\Delta_{*}(y)
for the diagonal
\Delta\colon H\hookrightarrow\bar{G}
,

•

a parabolic
Q\in\Par(G^{\circ})
with
\alpha(Q)=Q
and
(\bar{x},\bar{Q}):=(f_{*}(x),f(Q))\succeq 0
,

•

a subvariety
\mathfrak{H}\subset\Par_{t(x)}(G^{\circ})
such that the projection
\mathfrak{H}\to\Par_{t(\bar{x})}(\bar{G}^{\circ})
is a smooth proper morphism with equidimensional fibers.

Then the Zariski closure of
p([x,Q]_{\alpha})\cap\mathfrak{H}
in
\mathfrak{H}
has codimension
\geqslant n-\dim H
.

Proof.

Put
\varepsilon=(n-1)/N
, then (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) will be implied by the two inequalities

\displaystyle\varepsilon+\tfrac{1}{N}\leqslant\dim\Par_{t(y)}(H^{\circ}),

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{H,y}(\varepsilon)+\Upsilon_{H,y}(\varepsilon+\tfrac{1}{2}(\dim P_{y}^{\textup{ss}}-\rk H))<\max\Upsilon_{H,y}.

Since we fixed the dimension of
H
and the set of slopes note that there are only finitely many possibilities for
\Upsilon_{H,y}
,
\dim\Par_{t(y)}(H^{\circ})
,
\dim P_{y}^{\textup{ss}}
and
\rk H
. For
N
big enough the first of the above inequalities is always satisfied, and the second one is equivalent to
\tfrac{1}{2}(\dim P_{y}^{\textup{ss}}-\rk G)<\dim\rad P_{y}
: Indeed the function
\Upsilon_{H,y}
is increasing on the interval
[0,\dim\rad P_{y}]
by the formula for the adjoint polygon in terms of weights in (
6.3
) below; since
\dim\rad P_{y}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\dim H-\dim P_{y}^{\textup{ss}})
, the second inequality then becomes equivalent to
2\dim P_{y}^{\textup{ss}}<\dim H+\rk H
.
∎

The rest of this section will be devoted to the proof of
theorem

6.4
.

6.3.
Strategy of the proof

One easily reduces to the case where the group
G
is connected: Indeed, it is easy to see that
[x,Q]_{G,\alpha}
is finite union of sets
[x^{\prime},Q^{\prime}]_{G^{\circ},\alpha}
with
x^{\prime}=gx
and
Q^{\prime}=gQg^{-1}
for representatives
g\in G(k)
of
G(k)/G^{\circ}(k)
. Since moreover the assumptions of
theorem

6.4
only depend on the connected component of
G
, we will for the rest of the proof assume that
G
is
connected
. Extending scalars only makes the set
[x,Q]_{\alpha}
bigger, so we will also assume
k
to be
algebraically closed
and hence identify varieties with the set of their
k
-points.

Now recall that the definition of
[x,Q]_{\alpha}
involves two conditions: A positivity condition and a condition on
\alpha
-equivalence.
For the proof of
theorem

6.4
we will construct a morphism from
\Par(G^{\circ})\times\Par(G^{\circ})
to another variety such that the dimension of the image
[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}
of
[x,Q]_{\alpha}
will be bounded using
\alpha
-equivalence only; the dimension of the fibers will instead be bounded using positivity, for which the automorphism
\alpha
will no longer play a role.

To construct the morphism, let
\mathcal{Q}
be the universal parabolic subgroup over the variety of parabolic subgroups
\Par(G)
. Its reductive quotient
\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}}:=\mathcal{Q}/\rad\mathcal{Q}
is a reductive group scheme over
\Par(G)
with connected fibers. Therefore the scheme of its parabolic subgroups is represented by a smooth and proper
\Par(G)
-scheme

\Par(\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}})\longrightarrow\Par(G).

Let
Q\subset G
be a parabolic subgroup with
\alpha(Q)=Q
and
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Set

[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}\;:=\;\left\{(\Stab_{Q^{\prime\textup{ss}}}(y),Q^{\prime})\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0ptQ^{\prime}=gQg^{-1},y=\pr_{Q^{\prime}}(gx),\\
g\in G\textup{ with }g^{-1}\alpha(g)\in\rad Q\end{array}\right.\right\}\;\subset\;\Par(\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}}).

To see how this set is related to
[x,Q]_{\alpha}
notice that the set-theoretical map

r\colon\Par(G)\times\Par(G)\longrightarrow\Par(\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}}),\hskip 10.00002pt(P,Q)\longmapsto(P\cap Q/P\cap\rad Q,Q).

induces a map

r\colon[x,Q]_{\alpha}\longrightarrow[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}.

Indeed, for any parabolic subgroup
Q^{\prime}\subset G
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
,
lemma

3.11
gives

\Stab_{Q^{\prime\textup{ss}}}(\pr_{Q^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}))\;=\;\Stab_{G}(x^{\prime})\cap Q\;/\;\Stab_{G}(x^{\prime})\cap\rad Q.

Notice that if the couple
(x,Q)
is positive, then the map
r\colon[x,Q]_{\alpha}\to[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}
is surjective even though the definition of the target does not involve any positivity condition. We will bound the dimension of the target in terms of
\alpha
only:

Proposition 6.6
.

With notation as above, the Zariski closure of
[x,Q]^{\textup{ss}}_{\alpha}
in
\Par(\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}})
has dimension

\dim\overline{[x,Q]^{\textup{ss}}}\;\leqslant\;\dim C_{G}(\alpha^{\textup{ss}}).

The proof of this will be given in
section

6.4
. It then remains to bound the fibers of
r\colon[x,Q]\to[x,Q]^{\textup{ss}}
.
To do this, we consider for a parabolic subgroup
Q^{\prime}\subset G
the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(\bar{G},k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(Q^{\prime\textup{ss}},k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(\bar{Q}^{\prime\textup{ss}},k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{Q^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\mapsto\bar{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{\bar{Q}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\mapsto\bar{y}}

where
\bar{Q}^{\prime}
is the image of
Q^{\prime}
via the projection
G\to\bar{G}
. The fibers of the map
r
will be bounded in terms of the sets

[\bar{x}]_{\bar{Q}^{\prime},\bar{y}}\;:=\;\{P_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}\mid\bar{x}^{\prime}\in\bar{G}\bar{x}\textup{ is $\bar{Q}^{\prime}$-positive and $\pr_{\bar{Q}^{\prime}}(\bar{x}^{\prime})=\bar{y}$}\}\;\subset\;\Par(\bar{G})

for
y\in\mathcal{B}(Q,k)
. In
section

6.6
we will show:

Proposition 6.7
.

With notation as above, let
n\geqslant 1
be such that (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) hold.
Then the Zariski closure of
[\bar{x}]_{\bar{Q}^{\prime},\bar{y}}
in
\Par_{t(\bar{x})}(\bar{G})
has codimension
\geqslant n
.

Assume now that we have
Q^{\prime}=gQg^{-1}
and
y=\pr_{Q^{\prime}}(gx)
for some
g\in G
such that
g^{-1}\alpha(g)\in\rad Q
. Then
(P_{y},Q^{\prime})\in[x,Q]^{\textup{ss}}_{\alpha}
for
P_{y}=\Stab_{Q^{\prime\textup{ss}}}(y)
, and we have an inclusion

q\bigl(\,r^{-1}(P_{y},Q^{\prime})\cap[x,Q]_{\alpha}\,\bigr)\;\subset\;{[\bar{x}]}_{\bar{Q}^{\prime},\bar{y}}\times\{\bar{Q}^{\prime}\}

for the composite morphism
q\colon\mathfrak{H}\hookrightarrow\Par(G)\twoheadrightarrow\Par(\bar{G})
. The situation is summarized in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Par(\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[x,Q]^{\textup{ss}}_{\alpha}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Par(G)\times\Par(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[x,Q]_{\alpha}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{H}\times\Par(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{-1}(P_{y},Q^{\prime})\cap[x,Q]_{\alpha}\cap(\mathfrak{H}\times\Par(G))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Par(\bar{G})\times\Par(\bar{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{[\bar{x}]}_{\bar{Q}^{\prime},\bar{y}}\times\{\bar{Q}^{\prime}\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\times\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\times\id}

We are now in position to prove
theorem

6.4
assuming
propositions

6.6
and
6.7
:

Proof of
theorem

6.4
.

The set
[x,Q]_{\alpha}\subset\Par(G)\times\Par(G)
is constructible by
lemma

6.8
below, thus it suffices to show

\dim{[x,Q]_{\alpha}\cap(\mathfrak{H}\times\Par(G))}=\dim\mathfrak{H}-n+\dim C_{G}(\alpha).

By
proposition

6.6
the Zariski closure of
[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}
has dimension
\leqslant\dim C_{G}(\alpha)
. In general the set-theoretical map
r
is not underlying a morphism of varieties as the intersection of parabolic subgroups may jump. Nonetheless
\Par(G)\times\Par(G)
can be decomposed into a finite collection
\mathcal{S}
of pairwise disjoint locally closed subsets such that for each
S\in\mathcal{S}
the map
r\colon S\to\Par(\mathcal{Q})
is a morphism of varieties. Therefore for each point
(P_{y},Q^{\prime})\in[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}
the fiber

F:=r^{-1}(P_{y},Q^{\prime})\cap[x,Q]_{\alpha}\cap(\mathfrak{H}\times\Par(G))

is constructible and it suffices to show that
F
has dimension
\leqslant\dim\mathfrak{H}-n
. As mentioned above, we have

F\;\subset\;q^{-1}([\bar{x}]_{\bar{Q}^{\prime},\bar{y}})\times\{Q^{\prime}\}.

By
proposition

6.7
the Zariski closure of
[\bar{x}]_{\bar{Q}^{\prime},\bar{y}}
has codimension
\geqslant n
in
\Par_{t(\bar{x})}(\bar{G})
. By hypothesis the morphism
q\colon\mathfrak{H}\to\Par_{t(\bar{x})}(\bar{G})
is proper smooth with equidimensional fiber. Therefore the Zariski closure of
q^{-1}([\bar{x}]_{\bar{Q}^{\prime},\bar{y}})
has codimension
\geqslant n
, hence the Zariski closure of
F
in
\mathfrak{H}\times\{Q^{\prime}\}
has dimension
\leqslant\dim\mathfrak{H}-n
as desired.
∎

Lemma 6.8
.

The set
[x,Q]_{\alpha}
is constructible.

Proof.

The set
[x,Q]_{\alpha}
is the intersection of the sets

\displaystyle X\;

\displaystyle:=\;\{(P_{x^{\prime}},Q^{\prime})\mid(x^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\sim_{\alpha}(x,Q)\},

\displaystyle Y\;

\displaystyle:=\;\{(P_{x^{\prime}},Q^{\prime})\mid(\bar{x}^{\prime},\bar{Q}^{\prime})\succeq 0,x^{\prime}\in Gx\},

thus it suffices to show that
X
and
Y
are constructible. Now
X
is the image of the morphism

\{g\in G\mid g^{-1}\alpha(g)\in\rad Q\}\longrightarrow\Par(G)^{2},\hskip 20.00003ptg\longmapsto(gP_{x}g^{-1},gQg^{-1}),

and is therefore constructible. The subset
Y
is the preimage of

\bar{Y}\;:=\;\{(P_{\bar{x}^{\prime}},\bar{Q}^{\prime})\mid(\bar{x}^{\prime},{\bar{Q}^{\prime}})\succeq 0,\bar{x}^{\prime}\in\bar{G}\bar{x}\}\;\subset\;\Par(\bar{G})\times\Par(\bar{G})

under the projection
\Par(G)\times\Par(G)\to\Par(\bar{G})\times\Par(\bar{G})
, so it suffices to show
\bar{Y}
is constructible. From now on the group
G
does not play a role any more, hence to simplify notation we write
G
,
x
,
Q
,
Y
etc. instead of
\bar{G}
,
\bar{x}
,
\bar{Q}
,
\bar{Y}
etc. Then
Y
is the image of the morphism

G\times\{(P_{x^{\prime}},Q)\in Y\}\longrightarrow\Par(G)^{2},\hskip 20.00003pt(g,P,Q)\longmapsto(gPg^{-1},gQg^{-1}),

so it suffices to prove that
\{(P_{x^{\prime}},Q)\in Y\}\subset\Par(G)\times\{Q\}
is constructible. To do so let
x=[\tfrac{\lambda}{n}]
for a cocharacter
\lambda
with values in a Levi factor
L
of
Q
. For
u\in\rad Q
a point
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is
Q
-positive if and only if
ux^{\prime}
is. Since all Levi factors of
Q
are conjugates under
\rad Q
, this implies

\{(P_{x^{\prime}},Q)\in Y\}=\rad Q\cdot\{(P_{x^{\prime}},Q)\in Y\mid x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(L,k)\}.

By
proposition

A.1
the set
Gx\cap\mathcal{B}(L,k)
is a finite union of
L
-orbits, thus it suffices to show that for
y\in\mathcal{B}(L,k)
the set
\{P_{gy}\mid g\in L,(gy,Q)\succeq 0\}\subset\Par(G)
is constructible. Let
\rho\colon Q\to\GL(V)
be the adjoint representation on
V:=\Lie\rad Q
. Since the weight of a filtration on
V
is invariant under
\GL(V)
, we have

\wt(\rho_{\ast}(gy))=\wt(\rho(g)\rho_{\ast}y)=\wt(\rho_{\ast}y)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $g\in L$}.

So
\{P_{gy}\mid g\in L,(gy,{Q})\succeq 0\}
is either empty or an
L
-orbit, hence constructible. ∎

6.4.
Bounds on the base

Now we go back to the setting in
section

6.2
. For the proof of
proposition

6.7
we will first reduce to the case where the automorphism
\alpha
is semisimple; in this case we have the following result:

Lemma 6.9
.

Suppose
\alpha
semisimple. Let
\Par(G)^{\alpha}\subset\Par(G)
be the subvariety of parabolic subgroups stable under
\alpha
. Then
C_{G}(\alpha)
acts naturally on
\Par(G)^{\alpha}
and

|\Par(G)^{\alpha}/C_{G}(\alpha)|<+\infty.

Proof.

Let
Q\subset G
be a parabolic subgroup stable under
\alpha
and suppose that the corresponding point
[Q]\in\Par(G)^{\alpha}
is smooth. Consider the map
\sigma\colon G\to\Par(G)
defined by
\sigma(g)=[gQg^{-1}]
. It suffices to show that the tangent map

\textup{T}_{e}\sigma\colon\Lie G\longrightarrow\textup{T}_{[Q]}\Par(G)

induces a surjection
\Lie C\to\textup{T}_{[Q]}\Par(G)^{\alpha}
. Now
\textup{T}_{[Q]}\Par(G)^{\alpha}
is made of those tangent vectors to
\Par(G)
in
[Q]
that are fixed under the action of
\alpha
. Via
\textup{T}_{e}\sigma
we have an isomorphism

\textup{T}_{[Q]}\Par(G)\cong\Lie G/\Lie Q

of representation of
G
. Since
\alpha
is semisimple, the subspace of
\alpha
-invariants of
\Lie G
surject onto that of
\Lie G/\Lie Q
. The result follows as
\Lie C\subset\Lie G
is the subspace of
\alpha
-invariants.
∎

We will then pass from the centralizer in a parabolic subgroup to a centralizer in its Levi quotient as follows.
For a parabolic subgroup
Q\subset G
fixed under
\alpha
set
C_{Q}(\alpha):=C_{G}(\alpha)\cap Q
. The radical
\rad Q
is also stable under
\alpha
, hence
\alpha
induces an automorphism of the Levi quotient
Q^{\textup{ss}}:=Q/\rad Q
. Let
C_{Q^{\textup{ss}}}(\alpha)\subset Q^{\textup{ss}}
be the subgroup of fixed points of this automorphism, then we have:

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose
\alpha
semisimple. Given a parabolic subgroup
Q\subset G
stable under
\alpha
the image of
\pi_{Q}\colon C_{Q}(\alpha)\to C_{Q^{\textup{ss}}}(\alpha)
is a subgroup of finite index.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the tangent map
p=\Lie\pi_{Q}\colon\Lie Q\to\Lie Q^{\textup{ss}}
restricts to a surjection
\Lie C_{Q}(\alpha)\to\Lie C_{Q^{\textup{ss}}}(\alpha)
. The tangent action of
\alpha
on
\Lie Q
preserves
\Lie\rad Q
and the projection
p
is
\alpha
-equivariant. Since
\alpha
is semisimple, there is a unique complement
L
of
\Lie\rad Q
in
\Lie Q
which is stable under
\alpha
and the projection
p\colon L\to Q^{\textup{ss}}
is an isomorphism. The Lie algebra of
C_{Q}(\alpha)
consists of the vectors in
\Lie Q
fixed by
\alpha
, and similarly for
C_{Q^{\textup{ss}}}(\alpha)
. In particular
p
induces an isomorphism
\{v\in L:\alpha(v)=v\}\to\Lie C_{Q^{\textup{ss}}}(\alpha)
which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
proposition

6.6
.

The subset
[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}\subset\Par(\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}})
is constructible because it is the image of the subvariety

T:=\{g\in G\mid g^{-1}\alpha(g)\in\rad Q\}\subset G

via the morphism
T\to\Par(\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}})
defined by

g\;\longmapsto\;g(Q\cap P_{x})g^{-1}/g(\rad Q\cap P_{x})g^{-1}\;\subset\;(gQg^{-1})^{\textup{ss}}.

Here
g(Q\cap P_{x})g^{-1}/g(\rad Q\cap P_{x})g^{-1}
is the stabilizer of the point
\pr_{gQg^{-1}}(gx)
by
lemma

3.11
. Now we reduce to the case where
\alpha
is semisimple: Indeed, let
g\in G
such that
g^{-1}\alpha(g)\in\rad Q
, then

\{\beta\in\Aut(G)\mid\beta(Q)=Q,g^{-1}\beta(g)\in\rad Q\}\subset\Aut(G)

is an algebraic subgroup. By functoriality of the Jordan-Chevalley decomposition the semisimple part
\beta=\alpha^{\textup{ss}}
of
\alpha
belongs to such a subgroup and
[x,Q]_{\alpha}\subset[x,Q]^{\textup{ss}}_{\beta}
, so for the rest of the proof we can assume that
\alpha=\alpha^{\textup{ss}}
is semisimple. This being said, let

\pi\colon\Par(\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}})\longrightarrow\Par(G),\hskip 10.00002pt(P,Q)\longmapsto Q

be the structure morphism of the
\Par(G)
-scheme
\Par(\mathcal{Q}^{\textup{ss}})
. The set
[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}
is stable under
C_{G}(\alpha)
, thus so is its image

\pi([x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}})\subset\Par(G)

because
\pi
is
G
-equivariant. Moreover, since
Q
is stable under
\alpha
, each parabolic subgroup in
\pi([x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}})
is stable under
\alpha
. By hypothesis the automorphism
\alpha
is semisimple, thus

|\pi([x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}})/C_{G}(\alpha)|<+\infty

by
lemma

6.9
. Therefore it suffices to bound the dimension of
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{C})\cap[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}
for each
C_{G}(\alpha)
-orbit
\mathcal{C}\subset\pi([x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}})
. Fix
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
and note that

\dim\mathcal{C}=\dim C_{G}(\alpha)-\dim C_{Q^{\prime}}(\alpha).

With notation as in
lemma

6.10
the set
\pi^{-1}(Q^{\prime})\cap[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}
is a single
C_{Q^{\prime\textup{ss}}}(\alpha)
-orbit, hence a finite union of
C_{Q^{\prime}}(\alpha)
-orbit by the same lemma because we assumed
\alpha
to be semisimple. In particular

\dim\pi^{-1}(Q^{\prime})\cap[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}\leqslant\dim C_{Q^{\prime}}(\alpha).

Summing up, the fibers of
\pi\colon[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}\to\Par(G)
have dimension
\leqslant\dim C_{Q^{\prime}}(\alpha)
while its image has dimension
\leqslant\dim C_{G}(\alpha)-\dim C_{Q^{\prime}}(\alpha)
. Therefore

\dim{[x,Q]_{\alpha}^{\textup{ss}}}\leqslant\dim C_{G}(\alpha)-\dim C_{Q^{\prime}}(\alpha)+\dim C_{Q^{\prime}}(\alpha)=\dim C_{Q^{\prime}}(\alpha),

which concludes the proof.
∎

6.5.
Interlude on roots

Let
B\subset G
be a Borel subgroup and
T\subset B
a maximal torus. For a connected subgroup
H\subset G
containing
T
, a root of
H
is a nontrivial character
\chi\colon T\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
for which the vector subspace

(\Lie H)_{\chi}:=\{v\in\Lie H:\textup{ad}_{H}(t).v=\chi(t).v,t\in T(k)\}

is nonzero, where
\textup{ad}_{H}\colon T\to\GL(\Lie H)
is the adjoint representation of
H
. Let
\Phi_{H}
be the set of roots of
H
. For
\chi\in\Phi_{G}
the vector space
(\Lie G)_{\chi}
has dimension
1
. A root
\chi\in\Phi_{G}
is
positive
if it belongs to
\Phi_{B}
. If
\chi
is not positive, then
-\chi
is positive. A root is
simple
if it is positive and it cannot be written as the sum of two positive roots. Let
\Delta_{G}
be the set of simple roots and

\Delta_{H}:=(-\Phi_{H})\cap\Delta_{G}=\{\chi\in\Delta_{G}:-\chi\in\Phi_{H}\}.

Let
N
be the normalizer of
T
in
G
. The group
N(k)
acts by conjugation on the group of cocharacters
X_{\ast}(T)
and the group of characters
X^{\ast}(T)
of
T
. These actions are linear, extend naturally to
\textup{X}_{\ast}(T)\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and
\textup{X}^{\ast}(T)\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and factor through an action of the Weyl group
W=N(k)/T(k)
. The action of
W
respects the natural pairing: for
w\in W
,
x\in\textup{X}_{\ast}(T)\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and
\chi\in\textup{X}^{\ast}(T)\otimes\mathbb{Q}
we have
\langle wx,\chi\rangle=\langle x,w^{-1}\chi\rangle
. The set of roots
\Phi_{G}
is stable under the action of
W
. For
n\in N(k)
having image
w
in
W
, the group
nP_{x}n^{-1}=P_{wx}
only depends on
w
.

With this notation we are able to give an explicit expression for the adjoint polygon of a point
x
lying in the apartment
\mathcal{B}(T,k)=\textup{X}_{\ast}(T)\otimes\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Suppose that
B
is contained in
P:=P_{x}
. Then

\Phi_{P}=\{\chi\in\Phi_{G}:\langle x,\chi\rangle\geqslant 0\}.

Let
\textup{ad}\colon G\to\GL(\Lie G)
the adjoint representation and
\Lie G=\bigoplus_{\chi\in\Phi_{G}\cup\{0\}}(\Lie G)_{\chi}
the decomposition of
\Lie G
in isotypical components with respect to
T
. The filtration
F^{\bullet}\Lie G
defined by
\textup{ad}_{\ast}x\in\mathcal{B}(\GL(\Lie G),k)
is given, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
, by

F^{\alpha}\Lie G=\bigoplus_{\langle x,\chi\rangle\geqslant\alpha}(\Lie G)_{\chi}.

Let
\{1,\dots,\dim G\}\to\Phi_{G}\cup\{0\}
,
i\mapsto\chi_{i}
be a surjection such that
\langle x,\chi_{i}\rangle\geqslant\langle x,\chi_{i+1}\rangle
and

|\{i:\chi_{i}=0\}|=\dim(\Lie G)_{0}=\dim T.

With this notation, for all
t\in[0,\dim G]
, we have

(6.3)

\Upsilon_{G,x}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}\langle x,\chi_{i}\rangle+\langle x,\chi_{\lfloor t\rfloor+1}\rangle(t-\lfloor t\rfloor).

Thus the function
\Upsilon_{G,x}
is increasing on
[0,\dim\rad P]
, constant on
[\dim\rad P,\dim P]
and decreasing on
[\dim P,\dim G]
. In particular
\Upsilon_{G,x}
is nondecreasing on
[0,\dim P]
and
\max\Upsilon_{G,x}=\sum_{\chi\in\Phi_{P}}\langle\chi,x\rangle
.

We take profit of this setup to state a refinement of the Bruhat decomposition that will be used afterwards. Given parabolic subgroups
P,Q\subset G
containing the Borel subgroup
B
consider

W_{Q,P}=\{w\in W:w\Delta_{P}\subseteq\Phi_{B},w^{-1}\Delta_{Q}\subseteq\Phi_{B}\}.

As usual, for
n\in N(k)
, the double coset
QnP
only depends in the image
w\in W
of
n
and is denoted by
QwP
.

Lemma 6.11
.

With the notation above we have
G=QW_{Q,P}P:=\bigcup_{w\in W_{Q,P}}QwP
.

Proof.

See the discussion after (11.12) in
[
LV20
]
.
∎

6.6.
Bounds on the fiber

In this section we are going to prove
proposition

6.7
. Since only the group
\bar{G}
plays a role, we reset notation and let
G
be a reductive group over
k
. For
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
, a parabolic subgroup
Q\subset G
and
y\in\mathcal{B}(Q^{\textup{ss}},k)
we consider the set

[x]_{Q,y}\;:=\;\{P_{x^{\prime}}\mid x^{\prime}\in G(k)x\textup{ is $Q$-positive and $\pr_{Q}(x^{\prime})=y$}\}.

Let
n\geqslant 1
be an integer such that

(6.4)

\displaystyle n\leqslant\dim\Par_{t(x)}(G),

(6.5)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{G,x}(n-1)+\Upsilon_{G,x}(n-1+\tfrac{1}{2}(\dim P_{x}^{\textup{ss}}-\rk G))<\max\Upsilon_{G,x}.

We are going to prove that the Zariski closure of
[x]_{Q,y}
in
\Par_{t(x)}(G)
has codimension
\geqslant n
. Note that (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) imply

(6.6)

\Upsilon_{G,x}(n-1)+\Upsilon_{G,x}(n-1+\dim P_{x}-\dim Q)<\max\Upsilon_{G,x},

and it is rather this weaker inequality that we are going to use. Indeed
\Upsilon_{G,x}
is nondecreasing on
[0,\dim P_{x}]
and

n-1+\dim P_{x}-\dim Q\leqslant n-1+\tfrac{1}{2}(\dim P_{x}^{\textup{ss}}-\rk G)\leqslant\dim P_{x}.

Since
[x]_{Q,y}=[gx]_{Q,y}
for all
g\in G
we may suppose that
P:=P_{x}
and
Q
share a Borel subgroup
B
. Consider a maximal torus
T\subset B
, the normalizer
N
of
T\subset G
and the Weyl group
W=N/T
. In this setup we can adopt the notation introduced in
section

6.5
. The point
x
then belongs to the apartment
\mathcal{B}(T,k)\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Note that
N
acts on
\mathcal{B}(T,k)
by conjugation and the action factors through
W
because
T
is commutative. Since a parabolic subgroup coincides with its own normalizer, the map
\sigma\colon G/P\to\Par_{t(x)}(G)
,
g\mapsto gPg^{-1}
is injective. For
w\in W
set

C_{w}:=\sigma(Qn)\;\subset\;\Par_{t(x)}(G)

where
n\in N
maps to
w\in W
; this does not depend on the chosen
n
. With the notation of
lemma

6.11
we have that
\Par_{t}(G)
is the union of
C_{w}
for
w\in W_{Q,P}
. Now the subset

[x]_{Q}:=\{P_{x^{\prime}}\mid x^{\prime}\in Gx\textup{ is $Q$-positive}\}=\bigcup_{y\in\mathcal{B}(Q^{\textup{ss}},k)}[x]_{Q,y}

is stable under the action of
Q
. Therefore for
w\in W_{Q,P}
either

C_{w}\subset[x]_{Q}\hskip 20.00003pt\textup{or}\hskip 20.00003ptC_{w}\cap[x]_{Q}=\emptyset.

Moreover
C_{w}\subset[x]_{Q}
if and only if
wx
is
Q
-positive. Let
W_{Q,P}^{+}\subset W_{Q,P}
be the subset of
w
such that
wx
is
Q
-positive. With this notation we have

[x]_{Q,y}=\bigcup_{w\in W^{+}_{Q,P}}C_{w}\cap[x]_{Q,y}.

By contradiction suppose that the Zariski closure of
[x]_{Q,y}
has codimension
\leqslant n-1
. The above decomposition implies that there is
w\in W^{+}_{Q,P}
such that the Zariski closure of
C_{w}\cap[x]_{Q,y}
has codimension
\leqslant c-1
. Since

C_{w}\cap[x]_{Q,y}=\{P_{qwx}\mid q\in Q\textup{ such that }\pr_{Q}(qwx)=y\}\neq\emptyset

there is
q\in Q
such that
\pr_{Q}(qwx)=y
. Applying
lemma

3.11
below to
qwx
we obtain the identities

\dim\overline{C_{w}\cap[x]_{Q,y}}=\dim(\rad Q/\rad Q\cap P_{qwx})=\dim(\rad Q/\rad Q\cap P_{wx}),

where the latter equality comes from the isomorphism
\rad Q\cap P_{wx}\cong\rad Q\cap P_{qwx}
given by conjugation by
q
. The roots appearing in the isotypical decomposition of
\Lie\rad Q/\Lie(\rad Q\cap P_{wx})
are of the form
-\chi
for

\chi\in\Phi^{w}_{Q,P}=\{\chi\in\Phi_{G}\smallsetminus\Phi_{Q}:-w^{-1}\chi\not\in\Phi_{P}\}

because
\Lie\rad Q=\bigoplus_{\chi\in\Phi_{G}\smallsetminus\Phi_{Q}}(\Lie G)_{-\chi}
. Thus
\dim\overline{C_{w}\cap[x]_{Q,y}}=|\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}|
so that the hypothesis we are trying to prove fallacious becomes

(6.7)

\dim G/P-|\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}|\leqslant n-1.

Now consider the modular character
\delta\colon Q\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
of
Q
. When restricted to
T
we have

\delta_{\rvert T}=-\sum_{\chi\in\Phi_{G}\smallsetminus\Phi_{Q}}\chi.

The point
wx
is
Q
-positive thus
\langle wx,\delta\rangle\geqslant 0
by definition. Since
\langle wx,\delta\rangle=\langle x,w^{-1}\delta\rangle
this inequality can be rewritten as

(6.8)

\sum_{\chi\in(\Phi_{G}\smallsetminus\Phi_{Q})\smallsetminus\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}}\langle x,-w^{-1}\chi\rangle\geqslant\sum_{\chi\in\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}}\langle x,w^{-1}\chi\rangle.

The previous equality will lead to a contradiction. On one hand each summand on the left-hand side of (
6.8
) is positive: roots
\chi\in\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}
satisfy
-w^{-1}\chi\not\in\Phi_{P}
which is equivalent to
\langle x,w^{-1}\chi\rangle>0
. In particular by the explicit expression for the adjoint polygon given in (
6.3
) we have

\sum_{\chi\in\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}}\langle x,w^{-1}\chi\rangle\geqslant\max\Upsilon_{G,x}-\Upsilon_{G,x}(n-1).

because by (
6.7
) there at most
n-1
roots
\chi\not\in\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}
such that
\langle wx,\chi\rangle>0
. On the other hand each summand on the right-hand side of (
6.8
) is nonnegative: indeed roots
\chi\in(\Phi_{G}\smallsetminus\Phi_{Q})\smallsetminus\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}
satisfy
-w^{-1}\chi\in\Phi_{P}
thus
\langle x,-w^{-1}\chi\rangle\geqslant 0
. So (
6.3
) implies the trivial lower bound

\sum_{\alpha\in(\Phi_{G}\smallsetminus\Phi_{Q})\smallsetminus\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}}\langle x,-w^{-1}\alpha\rangle\leqslant\Upsilon_{G,x}(\dim G/Q-|\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}|).

By (
6.7
) we have
\dim G/Q-|\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}|\leqslant\dim P-\dim Q+n-1
. The inequality (
6.8
) then yields

\max\Upsilon_{G,x}-\Upsilon_{x}(n-1)\leqslant\Upsilon_{G,x}(\dim G/Q-|\Phi_{Q,P}^{w}|)\leqslant\Upsilon_{G,x}(\dim P-\dim Q+n-1),

because
\Upsilon_{x}
is nondecreasing on
[0,\dim P]
and
\dim P-\dim Q+n-1\leqslant\dim P
by the assumption
n\leqslant\dim G-\dim P
. This contradicts (
6.6
) and concludes the proof.
∎

7.
Reminder on period mappings

For convenience of the reader we collect in this section some general facts about differential Galois groups and period mappings that will be used in the proof of the main theorem. Let
S
be a smooth geometrically connected variety over a field
k
of characteristic
0
.

7.1.
Differential Galois group

Following Katz
[
Kat87
, 1.1]
an
algebraic differential equation
on
S
is a pair
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
made of a vector bundle
\mathcal{V}
on
S
and an integrable connection
\nabla
. To ease notation we denote the pair simply by
\mathcal{V}
if the connection is clear from the context. Let
\DE(S)
be the category of differential equations on
S
with arrows given by parallel
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-linear morphisms. The category
\DE(S)
is abelian and
k
-linear with internal homs and a tensor product given by the usual formula. This
makes
\DE(S)
into a Tannakian category. For
s\in S(k)
the functor
\omega_{s}\colon\mathcal{V}\mapsto\mathcal{V}_{s}
taking the fiber at
s
is a fiber functor for
\DE(S)
. For an algebraic differential equation
\mathcal{V}
on
S
let
\langle\mathcal{V}\rangle
be the smallest tensor full subcategory of
\DE(S)
containing
\mathcal{V}
.

Definition 7.1
.

The
differential Galois group

\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)
of
\mathcal{V}
with base point
s
is the Tannaka group of the Tannaka category
\langle\mathcal{V}\rangle
together with the fiber functor
\omega_{s}
.

The differential Galois group
\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)
is the algebraic subgroup of
\GL(\mathcal{V}_{s})
whose points with values in a
k
-scheme
S
are those
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-linear automorphisms
\varphi
of
\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{S}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{W}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{S})=\mathcal{W}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{S}
for all integers
n\geqslant 1
,
a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and algebraic subdifferential equations

\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}^{a,b}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}^{\vee\otimes a_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\otimes b_{i}}.

The differential Galois group is invariant under extension of scalars: given a field extension
k^{\prime}
of
k
, we have

\Gal(\mathcal{V}^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)\times_{k}k^{\prime}

where
(\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
is the pull-back of
\mathcal{V}
along
S^{\prime}:=S\times_{k}k^{\prime}\to S
and
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}(k^{\prime})
is the point deduced from
s
; see
[
Kat87
, Prop. 1.3.2]
.

Definition 7.2
.

For an
S
-scheme
f\colon T\to S
let
\omega_{T},\omega_{T,s}\colon\DE(S)\to(\mathcal{O}_{T}\textup{-mod})
be the fiber functors defined by
\omega_{T}(\mathcal{W})=f^{\ast}\mathcal{W}
and
\omega_{T,s}(\mathcal{W})=\mathcal{W}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{T}
. The
Picard-Vessiot principal bundle

\PV(\mathcal{V},s)
of
\mathcal{V}
with base point
s
is the
S
-scheme whose points with values in an
S
-scheme
f\colon T\to S
are isomorphisms
\omega_{T}\to\omega_{T,s}
of fiber functors on
\langle\mathcal{V}\rangle
.

A point
\PV(\mathcal{V},s)
with values in an
S
-scheme
f\colon T\to S
is an
\mathcal{O}_{T}
-linear isomorphism
\varphi\colon f^{\ast}\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{T}
such that

\varphi(f^{\ast}\mathcal{W})=\mathcal{W}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{T}

for all algebraic subdifferential equations
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}^{a,b}
with
a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
. This shows in particular that
\PV(\mathcal{V},s)
is representable by a closed subscheme of the frame bundle of
\mathcal{V}
. By design
\PV(\mathcal{V},s)
is a principal bundle under the action by composition of
\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)
. Applying the definition with
T=\PV(\mathcal{V},s)
gives a ‘universal’ isomorphism

u_{\mathcal{V},s}\colon p^{\ast}\mathcal{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{\PV(\mathcal{V},s)}

where
p\colon\PV(\mathcal{V},s)\to S
is the structural morphism. Consider the formal completion
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}
of
\mathcal{O}_{S,s}
and the natural morphism
\iota_{s}\colon\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}\to S
. The formal analogue of the Frobenius integrability theorem states that evaluation at
s
induces an isomorphism

(\iota_{s}^{\ast}\mathcal{V})^{\nabla}:=\Ker\iota_{s}^{\ast}\nabla\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{V}_{s}

of
k
-vector spaces. By Nakayama’s lemma it follows that the natural map

(\iota_{s}^{\ast}\mathcal{V})^{\nabla}\otimes_{k}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\iota_{s}^{\ast}\mathcal{V}

is an isomorphism of
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}
-modules. Combining the previous two isomorphisms we obtain a third one

(7.1)

\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}\colon\iota_{s}^{\ast}\mathcal{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{k}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}

which is parallel with respect to the connection
\id\otimes\textup{d}
on the target. It follows that for any
n\geqslant 1
, any
a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and any algebraic subdifferential equation of
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}^{a,b}
we have
\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}(\iota_{s}^{\ast}\mathcal{W})=\mathcal{W}_{s}\otimes_{k}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}
. Hence
\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}
can be seen as a morphism

\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}\colon\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}\longrightarrow\PV(\mathcal{V},s)

whose composition with the structural morphism
p\colon\PV(\mathcal{V},s)\to S
is
\iota_{s}
. Suppose that as an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{V}
comes with a filtration

\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}\;:\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{V}\;=\;\mathcal{F}^{0}\;\supset\;\mathcal{F}^{1}\;\supset\;\cdots\;\supset\;\mathcal{F}^{n}\;\supset\;\mathcal{F}^{n+1}\;=\;0,

where each graded piece
\gr_{i}\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{F}^{i}/\mathcal{F}^{i+1}
is locally free of rank
r_{i}
say. Then the filtration
u_{\mathcal{V},s}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
on
\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{\PV(\mathcal{V},s)}
determines a morphism of
k
-schemes

(7.2)

\varphi_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}\colon\PV(\mathcal{V},s)\longrightarrow\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})

where
\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})
is the variety of flags of
\mathcal{V}_{s}
of type
r=(r_{0},\dots,r_{n})
. The above morphism is equivariant under the action of
\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)
. We will be interested in the composite map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}\colon\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PV(\mathcal{V},s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}}

7.2.
The analytic picture

Suppose
k=\mathbb{C}
and let
S^{\textup{an}}
be the complex manifold associated with
S
. The previous constructions can be carried out in the analytic framework, giving rise to the Tannakian category
\DE(S^{\textup{an}})
of holomorphic differential equations. For a holomorphic differential equation we still write
\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)
for the corresponding Tannaka group and
\PV(\mathcal{V},s)
for the Picard-Vessiot principal bundle.
The sheaf of germs of horizontal sections
\mathcal{V}^{\nabla}:=\Ker\nabla
is a local system on
S^{\textup{an}}
, which can be seen as a finite dimensional representation of the topological fundamental group
\pi_{1}(S^{\textup{an}},s)
. This induces an equivalence of the category
\DE(S^{\textup{an}})
with the one of finite dimensional representations of
\pi_{1}(S^{\textup{an}},s)
. In particular, for a holomorphic differential equation
\mathcal{V}
the Tannaka group
\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)
coincindes with
algebraic monodromy group

\Mon(\mathcal{V},s)
, that is, the Zariski closure of the image of the monodromy representation

\rho_{\mathcal{V},s}\colon\pi_{1}(S^{\textup{an}},s)\longrightarrow\GL(\mathcal{V}_{s}).

Let
\nu\colon\tilde{S}\to S^{\textup{an}}
be a universal cover of the topological space
S^{\textup{an}}
and
\tilde{s}\in\tilde{S}
a point over
s
. The sheaf
\nu^{\ast}\mathcal{V}^{\nabla}
is constant, thus the evaluation at each
t\in\tilde{S}
induces an isomorphism

\Gamma(\tilde{S},\nu^{\ast}\mathcal{V}^{\nabla})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\nu^{\ast}\mathcal{V})_{t}=\mathcal{V}_{\nu(t)}.

In particular the natural morphism
\Gamma(\tilde{S},\nu^{\ast}\mathcal{V}^{\nabla})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{S}}\to\nu^{\ast}\mathcal{V}

is an isomorphism. Combining the previous isomorphisms we obtain a parallel isomorphism of holomorphic differential equations
\nu_{\mathcal{V},\tilde{s}}\colon\nu^{\ast}\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{S}}

with respect the connection
\id\otimes\textup{d}
on the target. The previous isomorphism can be seen as a holomorphic map

\nu_{\mathcal{V},\tilde{s}}\colon\tilde{S}\longrightarrow\PV(\mathcal{V},s).

whose composition with the structural morphism
p\colon\PV(\mathcal{V},s)\to S^{\textup{an}}
is
\nu
. The map
\nu_{\mathcal{V},\tilde{s}}
is equivariant under the natural action of
\pi_{1}(S^{\textup{an}},s)
on
\tilde{S}
and the action on
\PV(\mathcal{V},s)
defined by monodromy representation

\rho_{s}\colon\pi_{1}(S^{\textup{an}},s)\longrightarrow\Mon(\mathcal{V},s)=\Gal(\mathcal{V},s).

Suppose that as an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{V}
comes with a holomorphic filtration
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
whose graded is locally free. Via the isomorphism
u_{\mathcal{V},s}\colon p^{\ast}\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{O}_{\PV(\mathcal{V},s)}
the filtration
u_{\mathcal{V},s}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
on
\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{O}_{\PV(\mathcal{V},s)}
determines a holomorphic map

\varphi_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}\colon\PV(\mathcal{V},s)\longrightarrow\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})^{\textup{an}}

where
\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})
is the variety on flags of
\mathcal{V}_{s}
of type
r=(r_{0},\dots,r_{n})
. The previous morphism is equivariant under the action of the differential Galois group
\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)
and we consider the composite morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}\colon\tilde{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PV(\mathcal{V},s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})^{\textup{an}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\mathcal{V},\tilde{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}}

Remark 7.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset S^{\textup{an}}
be a simply connected neighbourhood of
s
. The preimage
\nu^{-1}(\Omega)
is a disjoint union of copies of
\Omega
indexed by points in the fiber
\nu^{-1}(s)
. Having fixed such a point permits to identify
\Omega
with an open neighbourhood of
\tilde{s}
and consider the ‘restriction’ to
\Omega
of the above maps:

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{V},\Omega,s}:=\nu_{\mathcal{V},\tilde{s}\rvert\Omega}\colon\Omega

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\PV(\mathcal{V},s),

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathcal{V},\Omega,s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}:=\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}\colon\Omega

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})^{\textup{an}}.

The analytification functor
\DE(S)\to\DE(S^{\textup{an}})
is exact, faithful,
\mathbb{C}
-linear and compatible with tensor product. In particular, for an algebraic differential equation
\mathcal{V}
we have closed immersions

\Gal(\mathcal{V}^{\textup{an}},s)\subset\Gal(\mathcal{V},s),\hskip 20.00003pt\PV(\mathcal{V}^{\textup{an}},s)\subset\PV(\mathcal{V},s)^{\textup{an}}.

Moreover, according to Deligne
[
Del70
, Theorem II.5.9]
it induces an equivalence of categories
\DE(S)^{\textup{rs}}\cong\DE(S^{\textup{an}})

where
\DE(S)^{\textup{rs}}\subset\DE(S)
is the full subcategory of algebraic differential equations with regular singular points. Therefore the above closed immersions are isomorphisms as soon as the algebraic differential equation
\mathcal{V}
has regular singular points.

Remark 7.4
.

Let
\Omega\subset S^{\textup{an}}
be a simply connected open neighbourhood
\Omega
of
s
. Consider the holomorphic map
\Omega\to\PV(\mathcal{V}^{\textup{an}},s)\subset\PV(\mathcal{V},s)^{\textup{an}}
where the first map is
\iota_{\mathcal{V}^{\textup{an}},\Omega,s}
. By construction, via the isomorphism
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}\cong\hat{\mathcal{O}}^{\textup{an}}_{S,s}
the formal germ at
s
of the preceding map is
\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}
.

7.3.
The case of variations of Hodge structures

Keep the notation introduced in the preceding paragraph. Let
\mathcal{V}
be a holomorphic differential equation on
S^{\textup{an}}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
a filtration on
\mathcal{V}
whose graded is locally free. Suppose that the triple
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
underlies a pure polarized integral variation of Hodge structures
\mathcal{H}
on
S^{\textup{an}}
. The associated the holomorphic map

\tilde{\Phi}:=\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}\colon\tilde{S}\longrightarrow\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})^{\textup{an}}

is called the
complex period map
.

Proposition 7.5
.

With the notation above,

(1)

the algebraic group
\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}
is reductive;

(2)

the subgroup of
P\subset\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}
stabilizing
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{s}
is parabolic;

(3)

the image of
\tilde{\Phi}
is a Zariski-dense subset of
\mathfrak{H}:=\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}/P
;

(4)

Let
Z\subset\mathfrak{H}
be a subvariety with
\dim S+\dim Z\leqslant\dim\mathfrak{H}

and
Z^{\prime}\subset\tilde{\Phi}^{-1}(Z^{\textup{an}})
an analytic irreducible component. Then
\nu(Z^{\prime})
is not Zariski-dense in
S
.

Proof.

(1) See
[
Del71
, th. 4.2.6]
.

(2) The statement remains unaffected by the changes of the base point
s
, thus by
[
And92
, th. 1]
we may assume that
G:=\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}
is a normal subgroup of the Mumford-Tate group
\MT(\mathcal{H}_{s})
of the Hodge structure
\mathcal{H}_{s}
. With the notation of
section

3.1
the parabolic subgroup of
\GL(\mathcal{V}_{s})
stabilizing
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{s}
is
P_{\mu}
where
\mu
is the Hodge cocharacter. By construction
\mu
is contained in
\MT(\mathcal{H}_{s})
thus it normalizes
G
. Therefore
\mu
induces by conjugation a cocharacter of
\Aut G
and in turn a cocharacter of
G/Z
by connectedness where
Z\subset G
is the center. Some multiple
\mu^{r}
then lifts to a cocharacter of
G
. Finally the subgroup
P\subset G
is the parabolic associated with the cocharacter
\mu^{r}
.

(3) By loc. cit. we may pick
s
such that
G:=\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}\unlhd\MT(\mathcal{H}_{s})^{\mathrm{der}}
is a normal subgroup in the derived group of the Mumford-Tate group. Up to isogeny any connected normal subgroup in a connected semisimple group splits off as a direct factor, so there is a connected semisimple subgroup
R\subset\MT(\mathcal{V},s)^{\mathrm{der}}
such that the morphism

m\colon\hskip 10.00002ptG\times R\;\longrightarrow\;\MT(\mathcal{H}_{s})^{\mathrm{der}},\hskip 10.00002pt(g,r)\;\longmapsto\;g\cdot r

is an isogeny. Fix a reference point
\tilde{s}\in\tilde{S}
above
s
. For any subgroup
M\subset\MT(\mathcal{H}_{s})
let
D(M)=M\cdot\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{s})\subset\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})^{\textup{an}}
denote the corresponding orbit in the flag variety. Then by
[
CMSP17
, prop. 15.3.13 and th. 15.3.14]
the isogeny
m
induces a surjective holomorphic map

D(G)\times D(R)\;\longrightarrow\;D(\MT(\mathcal{H}_{s}))

with finite fibers, and the period map factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})^{\textup{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(G)\times D(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\MT(\mathcal{V},s))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\Psi}

where the projection to the second factor
\pr_{2}\circ\Psi\colon\tilde{S}\to D(R)
is a constant map. So the image of
\tilde{\Phi}
is contained in a single
G
-orbit; it is then obviously dense in that orbit since it is stable under the action of
\pi_{1}(S,s)
and since by definition
G
is the connected component of the Zariski closure of the image of
\pi_{1}(S,s)
.

(4) This is a consequence of the Ax-Schanuel property for variations of Hodge structures
[
BT19
, th. 1.1]
. In order to apply it, set

\displaystyle X:=S,

\displaystyle\check{D}:=\mathfrak{H},

\displaystyle V:=S\times Z,

\displaystyle W:=\im(\nu\times\tilde{\Phi}\colon\tilde{S}\to S^{\textup{an}}\times\mathfrak{H}^{\textup{an}}).

Note that the closed analytic subspace
W\subset S^{\textup{an}}\times\mathfrak{H}^{\textup{an}}
coincides with the fibered product considered in
loc. cit.
By design the image of
Z^{\prime}
in
S^{\textup{an}}\times\mathfrak{H}^{\textup{an}}
via
\nu\times\tilde{\Phi}
is contained in some analytic irreducible component
U\subset V^{\textup{an}}\cap W
. If

\codim_{S\times\mathfrak{H}}U<\codim_{S\times\mathfrak{H}}V+\codim_{S\times\mathfrak{H}}W

then
\nu(Z^{\prime})
is not Zariski-dense by
loc. cit.
If this is not the case, the converse of the previous inequality yields

\dim S+\dim\mathfrak{H}-\dim U\geqslant\dim\mathfrak{H}-\dim Z+\dim\mathfrak{H}

because

\codim_{S\times\mathfrak{H}}U=\codim_{\mathfrak{H}}Z
and
\codim_{S\times\mathfrak{H}}W=\dim\mathfrak{H}
. Notice that the latter holds because the holomorphic map
\nu\times\tilde{\Phi}
has discrete fibers. The hypothesis
\dim S+\dim Z\leqslant\dim\mathfrak{H}
then yields
\dim U=0
forcing
Z^{\prime}
to be a singleton.
∎

7.4.
Back to formal

We go back to the case when
k
is an arbitrary field of characteristic
0
. Let
\mathcal{V}
be an algebraic differential equation over
S
and
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
a filtration on
\mathcal{V}
whose graded is locally free. With the notation of (
7.2
) consider the morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}\colon\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PV(\mathcal{V},s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}}

Definition 7.6
.

We say that the triple
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})

underlies a variation of integral pure polarized Hodge structures
if the differential equation
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
has regular singular points, and there are a subfield
k_{0}\subset k
finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
and an embedding
k_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

(1)

S
,
\mathcal{V}
,
\nabla
,
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
come by base change from objects
S_{0}
,
\mathcal{V}_{0}
,
\nabla_{0}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{0}
over
k_{0}
;

(2)

the triple
(\mathcal{V}_{0,\mathbb{C}},\nabla_{0,\mathbb{C}},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{0,\mathbb{C}})
underlies a variation of integral polarized pure Hodge structures on the complex manifold
S_{0,\mathbb{C}}^{\textup{an}}
.

When this is the case the morphism
\hat{\Phi}
is called the
formal period map
.

Proposition 7.7
.

Assume that
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
underlies a variation of integral pure polarized Hodge structures. Then,

(1)

the algebraic group
\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}
is reductive;

(2)

the subgroup
P\subset\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}
stabilizing
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{s}
is parabolic;

(3)

the scheme-theoretic image of
\hat{\Phi}
is
\mathfrak{H}:=\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}/P
;

(4)

let
Z\subset\mathfrak{H}
be a subvariety with
\dim S+\dim Z\leqslant\dim\mathfrak{H}
.
Then the scheme-theoretic image of
\hat{\Phi}^{-1}(Z)\hookrightarrow\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}\to S
is not
S
.

Proof.

Each statement is deduced from the corresponding one in
proposition

7.5
first by descending from
\mathbb{C}
to
k_{0}
and then extending from
k_{0}
to
k
.

(1) and (2) are deduced directly.

(3) Since the construction of the scheme-theoretic image is compatible with extension of scalars, we may assume
k=k_{0}
. In this case for ease notation we drop the subscript
0
. Let
\nu\colon\tilde{S}\to S^{\textup{an}}
be a universal cover of
S
and consider the complex period map
\tilde{\Phi}\colon\tilde{S}\to\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{C},s})^{\textup{an}}
. With the notation of
remark

7.3
for a simply connected open neighbourhood
\Omega\subset S^{\textup{an}}
of
s
let
\Phi\colon\Omega\to\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{C},s})^{\textup{an}}
be the restriction to
\Omega
of the complex period map
\tilde{\Phi}
. The Zariski closure of the image
\tilde{\Phi}
coincides with the Zariski closure of the image of
\Phi
. In turn this equals the scheme-theoretic image of the formal germ of
\Phi
at
s
. By
remark

7.4
the formal germ of
\Phi
at
s
is the morphism

\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{\mathbb{C}},s}\longrightarrow\Spec(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}\otimes_{k}\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{\Phi}_{\mathbb{C}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Flag(\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{C},s})

where the first is given by the natural injection
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}\otimes_{k}\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{\mathbb{C}},s}
and
\hat{\Phi}_{\mathbb{C}}
deduced from
\hat{\Phi}
by extending scalars to
\mathbb{C}
. It follows that the scheme-theoretic image of the formal germ of
\Phi
at
s
is the scheme-theoretic image of
\hat{\Phi}_{\mathbb{C}}
. We then conclude because the construction of the scheme-theoretic image is compatible with extension of scalars.

(4) Let
k^{\prime}_{0}\subset k
be a finitely generated extension of
k_{0}
over which
Z
is defined. The embedding
k_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
can be extended to
k_{0}^{\prime}
allowing to replace
k_{0}
by
k_{0}^{\prime}
and suppose
Z
defined over
k_{0}
. As in (3) then we can assume
k=k_{0}
and we borrow the notation introduced above.
Proposition

7.5
(4) implies that no analytic irreducible component of
\Phi^{-1}(Z_{\mathbb{C}}^{\textup{an}})
is Zariski-dense in
S_{\mathbb{C}}
. The preimage of
\hat{\Phi}^{-1}(Z)
via
\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{\mathbb{C}},s}\to\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}
is the formal germ of the finitely many analytic irreducible components of
\Phi^{-1}(Z^{\textup{an}}_{\mathbb{C}})
passing through
s
. Therefore we conclude again by compatibility of the scheme-theoretic image with extension of scalars.
∎

7.5.
The
p
-adic analogue

Suppose
k
is a complete valued extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
S^{\textup{an}}
denote the Berkovich analytification of
S
. Let
\mathcal{V}
an algebraic differential equation over
S
. Via the natural isomorphism
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}\cong\hat{\mathcal{O}}^{\textup{an}}_{S,s}

[
Ber90
, th. 3.4.1]
we can see
\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}
as the formal germ of an isomorphism between the
k
-analytic vector bundles
\mathcal{V}^{\textup{an}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{S}^{\textup{an}}
.

Lemma 7.8
.

The formal germ
\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}
converges on some open neighbourhood
\Omega\subset S^{\textup{an}}
of
s
to an isomorphism

\iota_{\mathcal{V},\Omega,s}\colon\mathcal{V}^{\textup{an}}_{\rvert\Omega}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}

which is parallel with respect to the connection
\id\otimes\textup{d}
on the target.

Proof.

Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{d}\in\mathcal{O}_{S,s}
be local parameters where
d=\dim S
. Since
S
is smooth the rigid-analytic analogue of the implicit function theorem assures the existence of an open neighbourhood of
s
on which the
z_{i}
define a
k
-analytic isomorphism with an open disc

\{x\in\mathbb{A}^{d,\textup{an}}_{k}\mid|t_{i}|<r_{i},i=1,\dots,d\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{d,\textup{an}}_{k}.

Here
r_{1},\dots,r_{d}>0
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{d}
are the coordinates on
\mathbb{A}^{d}_{k}
. Now
\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}
admits an explicit expression. Identify
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s}
with
k[\![z_{1},\dots,z_{d}]\!]
and for
i=1,\dots,d
consider the
k
-linear sheaf endomorphism
D_{i}:=\nabla(\partial/\partial z_{i})\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
. Then we have

\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}\colon\iota_{s}^{\ast}\mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{k}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{S,s},\hskip 20.00003ptv\longmapsto\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}D^{n}(v)_{s}\otimes\frac{z^{n}}{n!},

where for a
d
-tuple
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})
of non-negative integers we used the multi-index notation
z^{n}=z_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots z_{d}^{n_{d}}
,
n!=n_{1}!\cdots n_{d}!
and
D^{n}=D_{1}^{n_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ D_{d}^{n_{d}}
.
3
3

3

Notice that the order in the latter composition is irrelevant: the endomorphisms
D_{i}
commute because the derivations
\partial/\partial z_{i}
do and the connection
\nabla
is integrable.
The above power series has positive radius of convergence whence the existence of a neighbourhood
\Omega
as in the statement. The parallel character of
\iota_{\mathcal{V},\Omega,s}
is deduced from the one of
\iota_{\mathcal{V},s}
.
∎

Example 7.9
.

Suppose that
k
is a finite unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
\mathcal{O}_{k}
be its ring of integers. Let
S
be a separated smooth
\mathcal{O}_{k}
-scheme of finite type with generic fiber
S_{k}
,
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to S
a proper smooth morphism and
\pi\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
be a finite étale morphism. The vector bundle
\pi_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}
comes with a natural integrable connection
\nabla
induced by the canonical derivation on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Consider a subvector bundle
\mathcal{E}\subset\pi_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}
stable under
\nabla
. Let
i,j,q\geqslant 0
and suppose that coherent sheaves

\mathcal{V}:=\mathcal{H}_{\textup{dR}}^{q}(\mathcal{X}/S;\mathcal{E},\nabla)=\textup{R}f^{q}_{\ast}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{X}/S}\otimes\mathcal{E}),\hskip 20.00003ptR^{j}f_{\ast}(\Omega^{i}_{\mathcal{X}/S}\otimes\mathcal{E}),

are locally free, where
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{X}/S}\otimes\mathcal{E}
is the de Rham complex of
(\mathcal{E},\nabla)
. The vector bundle
\mathcal{V}
comes equipped with the Gauss-Manin connection that we still denote
\nabla
. Let
S_{\eta}\subset S_{k}^{\textup{an}}
be the
Raynaud generic fiber
of
S
. It is the compact subset whose points with values in a complete valued extension
k^{\prime}
of
k
are

\mathcal{S}_{\eta}(k^{\prime})=\im(\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{O}_{k^{\prime}})\hookrightarrow S(k^{\prime}))

where
\mathcal{O}_{k^{\prime}}\subset k^{\prime}
is the ring of integers. Let
\mathbb{F}
be the residue field of
k
and
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
that of
k^{\prime}
. The reduction map
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{O}_{k^{\prime}})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{\prime})
defines an anticontinuous map

\red\colon\mathcal{S}_{\eta}\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{S}}:=\mathcal{S}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{k}}\mathbb{F}.

A point
s\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{O}_{k})
being fixed, the open subset

\Omega:=\red^{-1}(\red(s))

is called the
p
-adic disc around
s
. It owes its name to the fact that formal smoothness of
\mathcal{S}
implies that
\Omega
is isomorphic as
k
-analytic space to some power of open unit disc. Comparison with crystalline cohomology shows that
\iota_{\mathcal{V},\nabla,s}
converges on the whole
\Omega
; see
[
Kat73
, prop. 3.1.1 and section 7]
.

Let
\Omega\subset S^{\textup{an}}
be an open neighbourhood of
s
on which it exists a parallel isomorphism
\iota_{\mathcal{V},\Omega,s}\colon\mathcal{V}^{\textup{an}}_{\rvert\Omega}\to\mathcal{V}_{s}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
as in the lemma. Then
\iota_{\mathcal{V},\Omega,s}
can be seen as a morphism
\Omega\to\PV(\mathcal{V},s)^{\textup{an}}
of
k
-analytic spaces. Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
comes with a filtration
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
whose graded is locally free. As usual this determines a morphism of analytic spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{p}:=\Phi_{\mathcal{V},\Omega,s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}\colon\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PV(\mathcal{V},s)^{\textup{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})^{\textup{an}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{V},\Omega,s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{V},s,\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}}}

When
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
underlies a variation of integral pure polarized Hodge structures in the sense of
definition

7.6
we call
\Phi_{p}
the
p
-adic period map
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
underlies a variation of integral pure polarized Hodge structures. Then,

(1)

the algebraic group
\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}
is reductive;

(2)

the subgroup
P\subset\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}
stabilizing
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{s}
is parabolic;

(3)

the Zariski closure of the image of
\Phi_{p}
is
\mathfrak{H}:=\Gal(\mathcal{V},s)^{\circ}/P
;

(4)

let
Z\subset\mathfrak{H}
be a subvariety with
\dim S+\dim Z\leqslant\dim\mathfrak{H}
and
Z^{\prime}\subset\Phi^{-1}_{p}(Z^{\textup{an}})
an analytic irreducible component. Then
Z^{\prime}
is not Zariski-dense in
S
.

Proof.

This is deduced by applying
proposition

7.7
to
k_{0}
and then extending scalars to
k
. As in the proof of
proposition

7.7
the point is that the formal germ of
\Phi_{p}
is the formal period map
\Phi
associated
(\mathcal{V}_{0},\nabla_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\bullet})
, or rather the extension to
k
of the latter. There is a little hiccup with (4): the
k
-analytic subspace
Z^{\prime}
may not contain
s
. In any case
Z^{\prime}
contains a point
s^{\prime}
defined over a finite extension
k^{\prime}
of
k
. We may replace
k
by
k^{\prime}
and
k_{0}
by a finite extension so that
s^{\prime}
is
k
-rational. In this case
\iota_{\mathcal{V},\Omega,s}
induces an isomorphism of
k
-vector spaces
\alpha\colon\mathcal{V}_{s}\to\mathcal{V}_{s^{\prime}}
. The
p
-adic period map
\Phi^{\prime}_{p}
centered at
s^{\prime}
is then
\beta\circ\Phi_{p}

where
\beta\colon\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s})\to\Flag_{r}(\mathcal{V}_{s^{\prime}})
is the isomorphism induced by
\alpha
. In particular
Z^{\prime}
is an analytic irreducible component of

\Phi^{\prime-1}_{p}(\beta(Z^{\textup{an}}))=(\beta\circ\Phi_{p})^{-1}(\beta(Z^{\textup{an}}))=\Phi_{p}^{-1}(Z^{\textup{an}}).

This permits to apply
proposition

7.7
(4) to the formal germ of
\Phi^{\prime}_{p}
and the subvariety
\beta(Z)
. Since the Zariski-closure of
Z^{\prime}
only depends on its formal germ, this concludes the proof.
∎

8.
The Lawrence-Venkatesh method: Proof of
D

We now put together the results from the preceding sections: We apply
p
-adic Hodge theory to a suitable étale datum and look at the arising
p
-adic period map to prove the main
D
from the introduction.

8.1.
Setup

Let
K
be a number field and
\Sigma
a finite set of places of
K
. Let
S
be a smooth integral separated
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
-scheme of finite type and
A
an abelian scheme of dimension
g
over
\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}
. Let
\mathcal{X}\subset A_{S}:=A\times S
be a closed subscheme which is smooth over
S
with geometrically connected fibers of dimension
d
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{S}}

8.2.
Numerics

Let
X
be a geometric fiber of
\mathcal{X}\to S
and

e=(-1)^{d}\chi_{\textup{top}}(X)

where
\chi_{\textup{top}}(X)
is the topological Euler characteristic of
X
. Consider the
split
reductive group
H
over
\mathbb{Q}
defined as

H=\begin{cases}\GL_{e}&\textup{if $X$ is not symmetric up to translation,}\\
\GO_{e}&\textup{if $X$ is symmetric up to translation and $d$ is even,}\\
\GSp_{e}&\textup{if $X$ is symmetric up to translation and $d$ is odd},\end{cases}

Let
y\in\mathcal{B}(\GL(W),\mathbb{Q})
be a point whose associated filtration
F^{\bullet}W
on
W=\mathbb{Q}^{e}
satisfies

\dim\gr^{\alpha}W=\begin{cases}(-1)^{d-\alpha}\chi(X,\Omega^{\alpha}_{X}),&\textup{if $\alpha=0,\dots,d$},\\
0&\textup{otherwise}.\end{cases}

This is possible since
h_{\alpha}:=(-1)^{d-\alpha}\chi(X,\Omega^{\alpha}_{X})\geqslant 0
by
proposition

B.3
. An explicit computation of
\dim P_{y}^{\textup{ss}}
in terms of the dimensions
h_{\alpha}=\dim\gr^{\alpha}W
case by case for each
H
shows the implication

\displaystyle 2\sum_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}^{2}=2(-1)^{d}\chi(\Omega^{d}_{X\times X})\leqslant\chi_{\textup{top}}(X\times X)=e^{2}

\displaystyle\;\implies\;

\displaystyle 2\dim P_{y}^{\textup{ss}}<\dim H+\rk H.

Let
N=N_{0}(\{0,1,\dots,d\},\dim S_{K}+\dim H,\dim H)\geqslant 1
be as in
corollary

6.5
.

8.3.
Construction of the étale datum

Let
A^{\natural}
be the moduli space of rank
1
connections on
A
and
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)
the universal rank
1
connection on
A\times A^{\natural}
. Up to enlarging
\Sigma
we may assume that the coherent sheaves over
A^{\natural}\times S
,

\mathcal{H}^{i}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{X}\times A^{\natural}/S\times A^{\natural};\mathcal{L},\nabla),\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{R}^{j}(\pi_{S}\times\id_{A^{\natural}})_{\ast}(\Omega^{i}_{\mathcal{X}\times A^{\natural}/S\times A^{\natural}}\otimes\mathcal{L}),

are locally free for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\pi_{S}\colon\mathcal{X}\to S
is the projection. If
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}
is not symmetric up to translation, we may replace
S
by a nonempty open subset and suppose that no fiber of
\mathcal{X}\to S
is symmetric up to translation.

Consider a friendly place
v
of
K
lying over a prime number
p
such that
\Sigma
contains no
p
-adic place. This is possible because there are infinitely many friendly places on
K
. Let
\bar{K}_{v}
be an algebraic closure of the
v
-adic completion
K_{v}
of
K
and
\bar{K}
the algebraic closure of
K
in
\bar{K}_{v}
. This induces an injection

\Gamma_{v}:=\Gal(\bar{K}_{v}/K_{v})\;\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\;\Gamma:=\Gal(\bar{K}/K).

Let
\mathbb{F}_{v}
be the residue field at
v
. The field
\mathbb{F}_{v}
is finite thus so is the set
S(\mathbb{F}_{v})
. In particular in order to prove that
S(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})
is not Zariski dense it suffices to prove that each fiber of the reduction map
S(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})\to S(\mathbb{F}_{v})

is not Zariski dense. Let

F\subset S(\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma})

be one of these fibers. Fix an embedding
\sigma\colon\bar{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and let
\pi_{1}(A_{\sigma},0)
be the topological fundamental group of
A_{\sigma}
. For a character
\chi\colon\pi_{1}(A_{\sigma},0)\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
let
L_{\chi}
be the corresponding rank
1
local system on
A_{\sigma}
. When
\chi
is torsion of order
r
it comes from a character
A[r](\bar{K})\to\mu_{r}(\bar{K})
where
\mu_{r}(\bar{K})\subset\bar{K}
is the set of
r
-th roots of unity. We let
\Gamma
act on such torsion characters via its action on
A[r](\bar{K})
and
\mu_{r}(\bar{K})
. By
[
LS20
, lemmas 4.8 and 4.9]
there is an integer
r\geqslant 1
nondivisible by
p
and a character
\chi_{0}\colon A[r](\bar{K})\to\mu_{r}(\bar{K})
of order
r
such that the following properties hold:

(1)

\textup{H}^{i}(\mathcal{X}_{s}(\mathbb{C}),\pi^{\ast}L_{\gamma\chi})=0
for all
\chi\in\Gamma.\chi_{0}
,
s\in S_{\sigma}(\mathbb{C})
and
i\neq d
;

(2)

for each
\chi\in\Gamma.\chi_{0}
there are
\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{N}\in\Gamma_{v}\chi
pairwise distinct such that
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\to A_{S,\sigma}
has big monodromy for the
N
-tuple
\underline{\chi}=(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{N})
.

Here
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to A
is the projection. Property (2) will be crucial to bound the dimension of the centralizer of the crystalline Frobenius; see the end of
section

8.6
and (
8.2
).
We see
A[r](\bar{K})
as a constant group scheme over
\mathbb{Q}
and consider its Cartier dual

A[r](\bar{K})^{\ast}=\Hom(A[r](\bar{K}),\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{Q}})

Any character
\chi\colon A[r](\bar{K})\to\mu_{r}(\bar{K})
induces a point of
A[r](\bar{K})^{\ast}
with values in the
r
-th cyclotomic extension of
\mathbb{Q}
. This permits to see
\Gamma.\chi_{0}
as a closed subscheme of
A[r](\bar{K})^{\ast}
whose geometric points are of the form
\chi\in\Gamma.\chi_{0}^{m}
for some integer
m
prime to
r
. We define
Z
to be the base change to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
of this closed subscheme. For each
s\in F
apply
example

4.3
with
X=\mathcal{X}_{s}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}}K
to obtain a
\Gamma
-étale datum

(V_{s},G_{s},\rho_{s})

on
Z
by restriction. For
s,s^{\prime}\in F
the couples
(V_{s},G_{s})
and
(V_{s^{\prime}},G_{s^{\prime}})
are isomorphic: Indeed, in the symmetric case Poincaré duality gives an isomorphism of local systems
V\to V^{\vee}
inducing the bilinear form
\theta_{s}
on the fiber over each point
s
, so the isomorphism type of the group preserving this bilinear form is independent of the point
s
. Fix a vector bundle
W
on
Z
, a reductive group
Z
-scheme
H
and for each
s\in\Omega
an isomorphism
(V_{s},G_{s})\cong(W,H)
. Let
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{B}(H,Z)/H(Z)
be a subset as in the statement of
lemma

4.8
. For each
s\in F
there is
x_{s}\in\mathcal{B}(G_{s},Z)
fixed under
\Gamma
and mapped in
\mathcal{I}
via the isomorphism
(V_{s},G_{s})\cong(W,H)
with the property that the graded of the filtered
\Gamma
-étale datum

D_{s}:=(V_{s},G_{s},\rho_{s},x_{s})

is semisimple.

8.4.
The Lawrence-Venkatesh strategy

By the version of Faltings’ finiteness of pure global Galois representations given in
corollary

4.7
, the image of the composite map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{filtered $\Gamma$-\'{e}tale}\\
\textup{data on $Z$}\end{array}\right\}/\cong}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{graded $\Gamma$-\'{e}tale}\\
\textup{data on $Z$}\end{array}\right\}/\cong}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[D_{s}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\gr D_{s}]}}
graded

is finite (here the brackets stand for the isomorphism class). So to show that
F
is not Zariski dense, it suffices to show that the fibers of the map

F\ni s\longmapsto[\gr D_{s}]

are not Zariski dense.
For this we continue the preceding diagram and apply
p
-adic Hodge theory to pass to the de Rham side:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{filtered $\Gamma$-\'{e}tale}\\
\textup{data on $Z$}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{graded $\Gamma$-\'{e}tale}\\
\textup{data on $Z$}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{filtered $\Gamma_{v}$-\'{e}tale}\\
\textup{data on $Z$}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{graded $\Gamma_{v}$-\'{e}tale}\\
\textup{data on $Z$}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{filtered de Rham}\\
\textup{data on $Z_{\textup{dR}}$}\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\textup{graded de Rham}\\
\textup{data on $Z_{\textup{dR}}$}\end{array}\right\}}}
restriction to
\scriptstyle\Gamma_{v}
graded
restriction to
\scriptstyle\Gamma_{v}
graded
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mapsto D_{\textup{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mapsto D_{\textup{dR}}}
graded

This is possible because
r
is not divisible by
p
, so the action of
\Gamma_{v}
on
Z
is unramified, and the ring
\Gamma(Z,\mathcal{O}_{Z})
is the product of unramified extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. It is then enough to prove that the fibers of the map

F\ni s\longmapsto[(\gr D_{s}\rvert_{\Gamma_{v}})_{\textup{dR}}]

are not Zariski dense, where for a filtered or graded
\Gamma
-étale datum
D
we wrote
D\rvert_{\Gamma_{v}}
for its restriction to
\Gamma_{v}
. Let
A^{\natural}
be the moduli space of rank
1
connections on
A
and let
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)
be the universal rank
1
connection on
A\times A^{\natural}
. By
example

5.15
we have that
Z_{\textup{dR}}
is a closed subscheme of
A^{\natural}_{K_{v}}
made of points of order
r
and

(D_{s}\rvert_{\Gamma_{v}})_{\textup{dR}}\cong(V_{\textup{dR},s}:=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{X}_{s}\times Z_{\textup{dR}}/Z_{\textup{dR}};\pi^{\ast}(\mathcal{L},\nabla)),G_{\textup{dR},s},\varphi_{s},h_{s}).

Recall that
\varphi_{s}
is the Frobenius operator acting on de Rham cohomology via the de Rham-crystalline comparison theorem. Moreover

G_{\textup{dR},s}=\begin{cases}\GL(V_{\textup{dR},s})&\textup{if $\mathcal{X}$ is not symmetric up to translation,}\\
\GO(V_{\textup{dR},s},\theta_{s})&\textup{if $\mathcal{X}$ is symmetric up to translation and $d$ is even,}\\
\GSp(V_{\textup{dR},s},\omega_{s})&\textup{if $\mathcal{X}$ is symmetric up to translation and $d$ is odd,}\end{cases}

where
\theta_{s}\colon V_{\textup{dR},s}\otimes V_{\textup{dR},s}\to\mathcal{L}_{a(s)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\textup{dR}}}
is the pairing induced by Poincaré duality if
\mathcal{X}=-\mathcal{X}+a
for some
a\colon S\to A
. With this notation
h_{s}\in\mathcal{B}(G_{\textup{dR},s},Z_{\textup{dR}})
is the point inducing the Hodge filtration on
V_{\textup{dR},s}
.

8.5.
Transliteration via parallel transport

Let
\mathcal{O}_{K,v}\subset K_{v}
be the ring of integers and
\mathbb{F}_{v}
the residue field of
K_{v}
. Consider the
\mathcal{O}_{K,v}
-scheme
S_{v}:=S\times_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\Sigma}}\mathcal{O}_{K,v}
and
S_{v,\eta}
the Raynaud generic fiber of
S_{v}
. As in
example

7.9
we see
S_{v,\eta}
as a compact subset of the Berkovich analytic space
S_{K_{v}}^{\textup{an}}
attached to the variety
S_{K_{v}}
over
K_{v}
. In this framework we have an anti-continuous reduction map

\red\colon S_{v,\eta}\longrightarrow\tilde{S}_{v}=S_{v}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{K,v}}\mathbb{F}_{v}.

Fix
o\in F
. The open subset
\Omega:=\red^{-1}(\red(o))
is such that

\Omega(K_{v})=\{s\in S(\mathcal{O}_{K,v})\mid s\equiv o\pmod{p}\}.

In particular
F
is contained in
\Omega
. The relative
d
-th de Rham cohomology group

\mathcal{V}:=\mathcal{H}_{\textup{dR}}^{d}(\mathcal{X}\times Z_{\textup{dR}}/S\times Z_{\textup{dR}};\pi^{\ast}(\mathcal{L},\nabla))

is a coherent sheaf over
S\times Z_{\textup{dR}}
and comes equipped with the Gauss-Manin connection
\nabla_{\textup{GM}}
. Actually note that
\mathcal{V}
is locally free by the choice of
\Sigma
at the beginning of the proof. For each
s\in F
, we have

\mathcal{V}_{s}:=\mathcal{V}_{\rvert\{s\}\times Z_{\textup{dR}}}=V_{\textup{dR},s}.

Applying the discussion in
example

7.9
over any point of
Z_{\textup{dR}}
the Gauss-Manin connection can be integrated over
\Omega\times Z_{\textup{dR}}^{\textup{an}}
to give an analytic isomorphism of vector bundles

\tau\colon\mathcal{V}_{\rvert\Omega\times Z^{\textup{an}}_{\textup{dR}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{V}_{o}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\Omega\times Z^{\textup{an}}_{\textup{dR}}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{where}\hskip 10.00002ptV:=\mathcal{V}_{o}=\mathcal{V}_{\rvert\{o\}\times Z_{\textup{dR}}}.

For
s\in F
the isomorphism of vector bundles
\tau_{s}\colon\mathcal{V}_{s}=V_{\textup{dR},s}\to\mathcal{V}_{o}=V_{\textup{dR},o}
is such that

G:=G_{\textup{dR},o}\;=\;\tau_{s}G_{\textup{dR},s}\tau_{s}^{-1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\varphi:=\varphi_{o}\;=\;\tau_{s}\varphi_{s}\tau_{s}^{-1}.

These identities come from the compatibility of parallel transport with Poincaré duality resp. with the isomorphism given by the de Rham-crystalline comparison theorem
[
BO78
, th. 7.1]
. The isomorphism
\tau
permits to identify
x_{s},h_{s}\in\mathcal{B}(G_{\textup{dR},s},Z_{\textup{dR}})
with points in
\mathcal{B}(G,Z_{\textup{dR}})
which we again denote
x_{s},h_{s}
. For each
s\in F
write
Q_{s}
for the stabilizer of
x_{s}
in
G
. This being set up, if
s,s^{\prime}\in F
are such that we have an isomorphism

(\gr D_{s}\rvert_{\Gamma_{v}})_{\textup{dR}}\;\cong\;(\gr D_{s^{\prime}}\rvert_{\Gamma_{v}})_{\textup{dR}}

of graded de Rham data over
Z_{\textup{dR}}
, then by
proposition

4.12
there is
g\in G(Z_{\textup{dR}})
such that

\displaystyle x_{s^{\prime}}=gx_{s},

\displaystyle g\varphi g^{-1}\varphi^{-1}\in\rad Q_{s^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\pr_{s^{\prime}}(h_{s^{\prime}})=\pr_{s^{\prime}}(gh_{s}),

where
\pr_{s^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{B}(G,Z_{\textup{dR}})\to\mathcal{B}(Q_{s^{\prime}}^{\circ,\textup{ss}},Z_{\textup{dR}})
is the projection. Notice that we can apply
proposition

4.12
because the subgroup
G\subset\GL(V)
is full. Let
\alpha
be the automorphism of
\Res_{Z_{\textup{dR}}/K_{v}}G
given by conjugation by
\varphi
. Then the above implies that the couples

(h_{s},\Res_{Z_{\textup{dR}}/K_{v}}Q_{s}^{\circ})\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{and}\hskip 10.00002pt(h_{s^{\prime}},\Res_{Z_{\textup{dR}}/K_{v}}Q_{s^{\prime}}^{\circ})

are
\alpha
-equivalent in the sense of
definition

6.2
. In order to apply
theorem

6.4
we still miss the positivity assumption on the above couples. For each
s\in S
the
\Gamma
-étale datum
D_{s}
is pure of weight
d
thus
proposition

5.17
implies that there is
z\in Z_{\textup{dR}}
such that

h_{s,z}\in\mathcal{B}(G_{z},k_{z})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{is positive with respect to}\hskip 10.00002ptQ_{s,z}^{\circ},

where
k_{z}
is the residue field at
z
. The point
z
depends a priori on
s\in F
, but in order to show that
F
is not Zariski dense we may suppose that it does not depend on
s
because
Z_{\textup{dR}}
is finite. In this case is suffices to show that the fibers of the map

F\ni s\longmapsto[(\gr D_{s}\rvert_{\Gamma_{v}})_{\textup{dR}}\rvert_{\{z\}}]

are not Zariski dense where for a graded de Rham datum
D
on
Z_{\textup{dR}}
we wrote
D_{\rvert\{z\}}
for its restriction to
\{z\}=\Spec k_{z}
.

8.6.
The period mapping

At this stage the scheme
Z_{\textup{dR}}
plays no role any more, thus in what follows we replace
Z_{\textup{dR}}
by the singleton
\{z\}=\Spec k
where
k=k_{z}
. Accordingly we have
G=G_{z}
,
V=V_{z}
, etc. from now on. For a variety
T
over
k
write
\Res T
for its Weil restriction to
K_{v}
whenever it exists. Notice that
S\times Z_{\textup{dR}}
then becomes the variety
S_{k}
over
k
obtained from
S
by extending scalars to
k
. Let
\mathcal{V}_{0}
be the push-forward of
\mathcal{V}
along the projection
S_{k}\to S_{K_{v}}
and
\nabla_{0}
be the connection on
\mathcal{V}_{0}
induced by the Gauss-Manin connection on
\mathcal{V}
. Let

\Gal(\mathcal{V}_{0},o)\subset\Res G

be the differential Galois group for the Gauss-Manin connection on
\mathcal{V}_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
be the Hodge filtration on
\mathcal{V}_{0}
. The pair
(\mathcal{V}_{0},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
underlies a variation of integral pure polarized Hodge structures
[
Del70
, th. II.7.9]
. By
proposition

7.10
(2) the subgroup
P\subset\Gal(\mathcal{V}_{0},o)^{\circ}
stabilizing the flag
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{o}
is a parabolic subgroup, hence we an inclusion

\mathfrak{H}:=\Gal(\mathcal{V}_{0},o)^{\circ}/P\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\Par_{t}(\Res G^{\circ})

where
t
is the type of the parabolic subgroup of
\Res G^{\circ}
stabilizing the Hodge filtration
h_{o}\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Consider the associated
p
-adic period mapping

\Phi_{p}\colon\Omega\longrightarrow\mathfrak{H}^{\textup{an}}.

By
example

5.6
the point
z
corresponds to the
\Gamma_{v}
-orbit of some
\chi\in\Gamma.\chi_{0}^{m}
with
m
an integer prime to
r
. With this in mind (plus the extension
k
of
K_{v}
being Galois) for a variety
T
over
k
we have a natural isomorphism

(8.1)

(\Res T)_{k}\cong T^{\Gamma_{v}\chi}

whenever the Weil restriction of
T
exists. By property (2) in the choice of
\chi_{0}
there are
\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{N}\in\Gamma_{v}\chi
pairwise distinct such that
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\to A_{S,\sigma}
has big monodromy for the
N
-tuple
\underline{\chi}=(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{N})
. In particular,

(8.2)

[k:K_{v}]=|\Gamma_{v}.\chi|\geqslant N.

Consider the projection onto the factors corresponding to
\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{N}\in\Gamma_{v}\chi
:

\pi\colon(\Res G)_{k}\longrightarrow\bar{G}:=G^{N}.

The hypothesis of having big monodromy implies that
\pi
induces a surjection

\Gal(\mathcal{V},o)_{k}\longrightarrow\hskip-14.0pt\longrightarrow\bar{G}.

Let
\bar{t}
the type of parabolics of
\bar{G}^{\circ}
induced by the type
t
of
G^{\circ}
. Then the composite morphism

\mathfrak{H}_{k}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\Par_{t}(\Res G^{\circ})_{k}\longrightarrow\hskip-14.0pt\longrightarrow\Par_{\bar{t}}(\bar{G}^{\circ})

is proper smooth with equidimensional fibers. For each
s\in F
let
h_{s,k}
be the point of
\mathcal{B}(\Res G,k)
induced by the point
h_{s}
of
\mathcal{B}(\Res G,K_{v})=\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Via the isomorphism (
8.1
) we have
\mathcal{B}(\Res G,k)=\mathcal{B}(G,k)^{\Gamma_{v}\chi}
and the point
x_{s,k}
is the diagonal embedding of
h_{s}
; moreover
h_{s,k}
is positive with respect to the parabolic subgroup

(\Res Q)_{k}^{\circ}\cong(Q_{s}^{\circ})^{\Gamma_{v}\chi}

because each coordinate of
h_{s,k}
with respect to this product decomposition is
Q_{s,k}^{\circ}
-positive. It follows that the point
\bar{h}_{s}\in\mathcal{B}(\bar{G},k)
induced by
h_{s,k}
is positive with respect to
\bar{Q}_{s}^{\circ}
where
\bar{Q}_{s}
is the image in
\bar{G}
of
(\Res Q)_{k}
. Let

F_{s}=\{s^{\prime}\in F\mid(\gr D_{s^{\prime}}\rvert_{\Gamma_{v}})_{\textup{dR}}\rvert_{\{z\}}\cong(\gr D_{s}\rvert_{\Gamma_{v}})_{\textup{dR}}\rvert_{\{z\}}\}

Now a point
s\in F
we have

\Phi_{p}(s)=P_{h_{s}}^{\circ}

as points of
\Par(G^{\circ})
, so the above discussion shows that
F_{s}
is contained in the preimage via the
p
-adic period mapping
\Phi_{p}
of the subset

E_{s}:=\pr_{1}([x_{s,k},(\Res Q_{s})_{k}^{\circ}]_{\alpha})\cap\mathfrak{H}_{k}

where
