\pr_{1}\colon\mathfrak{H}\times\Par(\Res G^{\circ})_{k}\to\mathfrak{H}
is the projection onto the first factor and, with the notation of
definition

6.3
,

[x_{s,k},(\Res Q_{s})_{k}^{\circ}]_{\alpha}:=\{(P_{x^{\prime}},Q)\mid(x^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\sim_{\alpha}(x_{s,k},(\Res Q_{s})_{k}^{\circ}),\;(\bar{x}^{\prime},\bar{Q}^{\prime})\succeq 0\}.

The slopes of the function
\Upsilon_{G,x_{s}}
lie in
\{0,\dots,d\}
and with the notation of
section

8.2
we have
\dim G=\dim H
and
\dim\Stab_{G}(x_{s})^{\textup{ss}}=\dim\Stab_{H}(y)
by
proposition

B.3
. Let
C
be the subgroup of
\Res G
of elements fixed by the semisimplification of
\alpha
. Then by
[
LS20
, lemma 5.33]
we have

\dim C\leqslant\dim G=\frac{\dim\Res G}{[k:K_{v}]}.

By (
8.2
) the extension
k
of
K_{v}
has degree
\geqslant N
. The choice of
N
implies that we can apply
corollary

6.5
to
\Res G
with
n=\dim S_{K}+\dim H
and obtain that the Zariski closure of
E_{s}
in
\mathfrak{H}_{k}
has codimension
\geqslant\dim S_{K}
. The
p
-adic version of the Bakker-Tsimerman theorem given in
proposition

7.10
(4) implies that

F_{s}\subset\Phi_{p}^{-1}(E_{s})\;\subset\;S_{v}

is not Zariski-dense. This concludes the proof. ∎

Appendix A
Conjugacy classes of disconnected reductive pairs

In this appendix we verify the fact about conjugacy classes that was used in the proof of
proposition

4.6
. Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. To simplify notation, we identify algebraic varieties over
k
with the set of their
k
-rational points. Let
V
be a finite dimensional
k
-vector space. For algebraic subgroups
H\subset G\subset\GL(V)
consider the set

\mathcal{C}_{G}(H):=\{H^{\prime}\subset G\mid H^{\prime}=gHg^{-1}\;\text{for some}\;g\in\GL(V)\}.

The group
G
acts naturally on
\mathcal{C}_{G}(H)
by conjugation.

Proposition A.1
.

If
G
and
H
are reductive, then the set
\mathcal{C}_{G}(H)/G
is finite.

Proof.

When
H
is finite this is
[
Vin96
, Cor. 16.2]
. We next deal with the case when
H
is connected: Then
\mathcal{C}_{G}(H)=\mathcal{C}_{G^{\circ}}(H)
, hence we may assume that
G
is also connected; in this case it suffices to show for the adjoint representation
ad
of
\GL(V)
that

\{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\mid\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}=\textup{ad}(g).\Lie H\subset\Lie G,g\in\GL(V)\}

is a finite union of
G
-orbits, which holds by a result of Richardson
[
Ric67
, th. 7.1]
.

When
H
is arbitrary, we combine the previous two cases as follows. By
lemma

A.2
below, there is a finite subgroup
F\subset G
such that the projection
F\to G/G^{\circ}
is surjective. From the already proven result in the connected case we know that the set
\mathcal{C}_{G}(H^{\circ})
consists of finitely many
G
-orbits. Fix such an
G
-orbit, say the
G
-orbit of
g_{0}H^{\circ}g_{0}^{-1}\subset G
. Writing
H^{\prime}=g_{0}Hg_{0}^{-1}
it suffices to prove that the set

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:=\{gH^{\prime}g^{-1}\mid g\in N_{\GL(V)}(H^{\prime\circ}),gH^{\prime}g^{-1}\subset G\}

consists of finitely many
N_{G}(H^{\prime\circ})
-orbits, where
N_{G}(H^{\prime\circ})
resp.
N_{\GL(V)}(H^{\prime\circ})
is the normalizer of
H^{\prime\circ}
in
G
resp.
\GL(V)
. Replacing
H
by
H^{\prime}
we may assume
H=H^{\prime}
. Now for
g\in N_{\GL(V)}(H^{\circ})
we have
gHg^{-1}=\langle H^{\circ},gFg^{-1}\rangle
because
H=\langle H^{\circ},F\rangle
. Since the connected component
H^{\circ}
is contained in
G
, we have that

gHg^{-1}\subset G\iff gFg^{-1}\subset G\iff gFg^{-1}\subset N_{G}(H^{\circ}).

The latter equivalence holds because
g
and
F
are contained in
N_{\GL(V)}(H^{\circ})
: the first by assumption and the second because any subgroup of
H
normalizes the connected component
H^{\circ}
. It follows that we have an inclusion

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\{gFg^{-1}\mid g\in N_{\GL(V)}(H^{\circ}),gFg^{-1}\subset N_{G}(H^{\circ})\}\subset\mathcal{C}_{N_{G}(H^{\circ})}(F),

yielding an injection
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}/N_{G}(H^{\circ})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{N_{G}(H^{\circ})}(F)/N_{G}(H^{\circ})
.
The group
N_{G}(H^{\circ})
is reductive and
F
is finite, thus
\mathcal{C}_{N_{G}(H^{\circ})}(F)
is a finite union of
N_{G}(H^{\circ})
-orbits by the finite case. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Given a reductive group
G
, there is a finite subgroup
F\subset G
such that the projection
F\to G/G^{\circ}
is surjective.

Proof.

We reproduce the argument
in
[
use13
]
.
First we reduce to the case when
G^{\circ}
is a torus: Let
T\subset G^{\circ}
be a maximal torus and
N_{G}(T)\subset G
the normalizer of
T
. The projection
N_{G}(T)\to G/G^{\circ}
is surjective. Indeed given
g\in G
the subgroup
gTg^{-1}
is a maximal torus of
G^{\circ}
. Since maximal (split) tori of a connected reductive group are all conjugated, there is
g_{0}\in G^{\circ}
such that
gTg^{-1}=g_{0}Tg_{0}^{-1}
hence
h=g_{0}^{-1}g
lies in the normalizer of
T
. In particular
g\equiv h
modulo
G^{\circ}
. Since
N_{G}(T)/T
is finite, we may replace
G^{\circ}
by
T
and
G
by
N_{G}(T)
. In this case write
T=G^{\circ}
and
W=G/G^{\circ}
. We claim that the extension
1\to T\to G\to W\to 1
is the pushout of an extension

1\longrightarrow T[n]\longrightarrow\tilde{G}\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow 1

along
T[n]\hookrightarrow T
where
n=|W|
and
T[n]\subset T
is the
n
-torsion subgroup. Since the group
\tilde{G}
is finite, the subgroup
F:=\im(\tilde{G}\to G)
is finite and does the job. To prove the claim, remark that
T
is commutative so we can see it as a
W
-module by conjugation. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
the short exact sequence
1\to T[n]\to T\to T\to 1
of
W
-modules induces an exact sequence

\Ext^{1}(W,T[m])\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}(W,T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Ext^{1}(W,T)

of abelian groups. These abelian groups are
n
-torsion because
n=|W|
. Thus by taking
m=n
one sees that the isomorphism class of the extension
[G]\in\Ext^{1}(W,T)
is in the image of
\Ext^{1}(W,T[m])\to\Ext^{1}(W,T)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
The Hodge filtration on twisted cohomology

In this appendix we gather some general facts about the Hodge filtration on the cohomology of torsion local systems of rank one. Let
Y
be a smooth projective complex algebraic variety with a free action of a finite abelian group
G
, and denote by
\pi\colon Y\to X=Y/G
the quotient. For
\chi\in G^{\ast}=\Hom(G,\mathbb{G}_{m})
, we denote by
L_{\chi}
the rank one local system on
Y(\mathbb{C})
whose sections on an open subset
U\subset Y(\mathbb{C})
are the locally constant functions
f\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\to\mathbb{C}
such that

f(gx)=\chi(g)f(x)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all}\hskip 10.00002ptg\in G,x\in\pi^{-1}(U).

We have a direct sum decomposition
\pi_{\ast}\mathbb{C}_{Y}=\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}L_{\chi}
inducing for each
q\in\mathbb{N}
a decomposition

\textup{H}^{q}(Y,\mathbb{C})=\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\textup{H}^{q}(X,L_{\chi}).

Similarly, let
\mathcal{L}_{\chi}
be the line bundle on
X
whose sections on an open subset
U\subset X
are those
f\in\Gamma(\pi^{-1}(U,\mathcal{O}_{Y}))
with
\sigma^{\ast}f=\chi\otimes f
for the group action
\sigma\colon G\times Y\to Y
. Then we have a decomposition

\pi_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y}=\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\mathcal{L}_{\chi}.

Proposition B.1
.

For
q\in\mathbb{N}
and any character
\chi\in G^{\ast}
, the Hodge decomposition of
\textup{H}^{q}(Y,\mathbb{C})
induces a decomposition

\textup{H}^{q}(X,L_{\chi})=\bigoplus_{i+j=q}\textup{H}^{j}(X,\Omega_{X}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi}).

Proof.

For every coherent vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
on
X
and
j\geqslant 0
, the projection formula shows that

\textup{H}^{j}(Y,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E})=\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\textup{H}^{j}(X,\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi}).

Taking
\mathcal{E}=\Omega_{Y}^{i}
and using that
\pi^{\ast}\Omega^{i}_{X}\to\Omega^{i}_{Y}
is an isomorphism because
\pi
is finite étale, we get

\textup{H}^{j}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{i})=\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\textup{H}^{j}(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi}).

We now insert this in the Hodge decomposition

\alpha\colon\textup{H}^{q}(Y,\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{i+j=q}\textup{H}^{j}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{i}).

The group
G
acts linearly on
\textup{H}^{q}(Y,\mathbb{C})
and
\textup{H}^{j}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{i})
, and
\alpha
is
G
-equivariant for these actions since the Hodge decomposition is functorial. The claim then follows by looking at eigenspaces: For
\chi\in G^{\ast}
, the
\chi
-eigenspace of
\textup{H}^{q}(Y,\mathbb{C})
is
\textup{H}^{q}(X,L_{\chi})
, while the
\chi
-eigenspace of
\textup{H}^{j}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{i})
is
\textup{H}^{j}(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi})
.
∎

Let
F^{\bullet}\textup{H}^{q}(Y,\mathbb{C})
be the Hodge filtration of
\textup{H}^{q}(Y,\mathbb{C})
, given in terms of the Hodge decomposition by

F^{p}\textup{H}^{q}(Y,\mathbb{C})=\bigoplus_{i=p}^{q}\textup{H}^{q-i}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{i}).

Proposition

B.1
shows that for
\chi\in G^{\ast}
, the induced Hodge filtration of
\textup{H}^{q}(X,L_{\chi})
is given by

F^{p}\textup{H}^{q}(X,L_{\chi})=\bigoplus_{i=p}^{q}\textup{H}^{q-i}(Y,\Omega_{X}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi}).

We are interested in situation where generic vanishing applies:

Example B.2
.

Consider a smooth complex projective variety
X
of dimension
d
with a morphism
f\colon X\to A
to an abelian variety. Recall that
f
is
semismall
if

\dim X\times_{A}X\leqslant\dim X.

For instance this is the case if
f
is finite. By
[
dCM09
, prop. 4.2.1]
the morphism
f
is semismall if and only if
P=Rf_{*}\mathbb{C}_{X}[d]
is a perverse sheaf, because
X
is smooth and proper.
Semismall morphisms are in particular generically finite. For our purpose we can weaken this as follows: Let us say that
f\colon X\to A
is
cohomologically semismall
if for all
i\neq 0
the perverse cohomology sheaves

{{}^{p}\hskip-1.99997pt\mathcal{H}}^{i}(P)

have Euler characteristic zero. In this case, generic vanishing
[
KW15
,
Sch15
]
says that most rank one local systems
L
on
A(\mathbb{C})
satisfy

\textup{H}^{q}(X,f^{*}L)\;=\;\textup{H}^{q-d}(A,P\otimes L)\;=\;0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all}\hskip 10.00002ptq\;\neq\;d.

If
L
is of finite order, then
L
embeds as a direct summand in the direct image
p_{*}\mathbb{C}_{B}
for some isogeny
p\colon B\to A
of abelian varieties. We can then place ourselves in the setting discussed at the beginning of this section by writing the given variety as the quotient
X=Y/G
of the smooth variety
Y=X\times_{A}B
by the free action of the finite abelian group
G=\Ker(p)
. Then
f^{*}L\cong L_{\chi}
for some character
\chi\in G^{\ast}
and therefore

\textup{H}^{q}(X,L_{\chi})\;=\;0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all}\hskip 10.00002ptq\;\neq\;d.

In general, such a vanishing property can be used to write Hodge numbers as Euler characteristics of sheaves of differential forms:

Proposition B.3
.

Let
d:=\dim X
and let
\chi\in G^{\ast}
be such that
\textup{H}^{q}(X,L_{\chi})=0
for all
q\neq d
. Then for
i=0,\dots,d
we have:

(1)

\displaystyle\dim\textup{H}^{d-i}(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d-i}\chi(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}),

(2)

\displaystyle\dim\textup{H}^{d-i}(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi})

\displaystyle=\dim\textup{H}^{i}(X,\Omega^{d-i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi}),

(3)

\displaystyle\dim\textup{H}^{d}(X,L_{\chi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{d}(-1)^{d-i}\chi(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}).

Proof.

(1)
\textup{H}^{q}(X,L_{\chi})=0
for
q\neq d
implies
\textup{H}^{j}(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi})=0
for
i+j\neq d
by
proposition

B.1
. In particular,

\displaystyle\chi(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(-1)^{j}\dim\textup{H}^{j}(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d-i}\dim\textup{H}^{d-i}(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi}).

But
\chi(X,\Omega^{i}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi})=\chi(X,\Omega^{i}_{X})
by Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch since
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{\chi})=0
.

(2) This follows from (1) since Serre duality gives

\chi(X,\Omega^{i}_{X})=(-1)^{d}\chi(X,\Omega^{d-i}_{X})
.

(3) This follows from (1) together with
proposition

B.1
.
∎

In the situation of
proposition

B.3
the Hodge filtration is compatible with the Poincaré pairing. To explain what we mean by this, note that the Poincaré pairing induces a perfect pairing

\theta\colon\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{H}^{d}(X,L_{\chi})\otimes\textup{H}^{d}(X,L_{\chi^{-1}})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}.

Corollary B.4
.

Let
\chi\in G^{\ast}
be a character with
H^{q}(X,L_{\chi})=0
for all
q\neq d
. Then the orthocomplement of
F^{i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi})
with respect to the bilinear form
\theta
is

(F^{i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi}))^{\bot}=F^{d+1-i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi^{-1}}).

Proof.

By construction of the Poincaré pairing we have for each
p,q
a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{d}(X,L_{\chi})\otimes H^{d}(X,L_{\chi^{-1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{d-p}(X,\Omega_{X}^{p}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi})\otimes H^{d-q}(X,\Omega_{X}^{q}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi^{-1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2d-p-q}(X,\Omega_{X}^{p+q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smile}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tr}

where
\mathrm{tr}
is the trace map if
p+q=d
, and the zero map otherwise. Hence the pairing between

F^{i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi})\;=\;\bigoplus_{p\geqslant i}H^{d-p}(X,\Omega_{X}^{p}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi})

and

F^{d+1-i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi^{-1}})\;=\;\bigoplus_{q\geqslant d+1-i}H^{d-q}(X,\Omega_{X}^{q}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\chi^{-1}})

is zero since it only involves terms with
p+q\geqslant i+d+1-i>d
. So we have an inclusion

F^{d+1-i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi^{-1}})\;\subset\;(F^{i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi}))^{\bot}

and to show that the two spaces are equal, it suffices to check that they have the same dimension. Since
\theta
is nondegenerate, the dimension of the orthocomplement is given by

\displaystyle\dim(F^{i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi}))^{\bot}

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\dim H^{d}(X,L_{\chi})-\dim F^{i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi})

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{i-1}(-1)^{d-p}\chi(X,\Omega_{X}^{p})

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{q=d+1-i}^{d}(-1)^{q}\chi(X,\Omega_{X}^{d-q})

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{q=d+1-i}^{d}(-1)^{d-q}\chi(X,\Omega_{X}^{q})

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\dim F^{d+1-i}H^{d}(X,L_{\chi^{-1}})

where the last four equalities are due to
proposition

B.3
.
∎

Note that part (1) of
proposition

B.3
in particular says
(-1)^{d-p}\chi(X,\Omega_{X}^{p})\geqslant 0
for all
p
, in line with the result for subvarieties of abelian varieties given by generic vanishing
[
PS13
, cor. 1.4]
. This allows to verify the numerical assumption (
1.1
) from the introduction for odd-dimensional varieties and for surfaces with nef cotangent bundle:

Lemma B.5
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
d
over a field of characteristic zero, and assume
h_{p}:=(-1)^{d-p}\chi(X,\Omega_{X}^{p})\geqslant 0
for all
p
. Suppose either that
d
is odd or that
X
is a surface with nef cotangent bundle. Then

2(-1)^{d}\chi(X\times X,\Omega^{d}_{X\times X})\;\leqslant\;\chi_{\textup{top}}(X\times X).

Proof.

Serre duality says
h_{p}=h_{d-p}
for all
p
, so the Künneth decomposition for differential forms gives

\displaystyle(-1)^{d}\chi(X\times X,\Omega_{X\times X}^{d})

\displaystyle\;=\;\sum_{p=0}^{d}(-1)^{d-p}\chi(X,\Omega_{X}^{p})(-1)^{p}\chi(X,\Omega_{X}^{d-p})

\displaystyle\;=\;\sum_{p=0}^{d}h_{p}h_{d-p}\;=\;\sum_{p=0}^{d}h_{p}^{2}.

On the other hand, the Hodge decomposition gives
\chi_{\textup{top}}(X)=h_{0}+\cdots+h_{d}
and therefore

\chi_{\textup{top}}(X\times X)\;=\;\left(\chi_{\textup{top}}(X)\right)^{2}\;=\;\left(h_{0}+\cdots+h_{p}\right)^{2}

It will thus be enough to show that the inequality
Q(h_{0},\dots,h_{d})\leqslant 0
holds for the quadratic form

Q(X_{0},\dots,X_{d})=\left(X_{0}+\cdots+X_{d}\right)^{2}-2(X_{0}^{2}+\cdots+X_{d}^{2}).

If
d
is odd, then the desired inequality follows from the relations
h_{i}=h_{d-i}\geqslant 0
since in this case

Q(h_{0},\dots,h_{d})=8\sum_{0\leqslant i<j\leqslant d/2}h_{i}h_{j}\geqslant 0.

If
d
is even, then one gets

Q(h_{0},\dots,h_{d})=-h_{d/2}^{2}+4(h_{0}+\cdots+h_{d/2-1})h_{d/2}+8\sum_{0\leqslant i<j\leqslant d/2-1}h_{i}h_{j}.

In particular, for
d=2
the desired inequality can be reformulated as follows:

\displaystyle Q(h_{0},h_{1},h_{2})\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;

\displaystyle h_{1}(4h_{0}-h_{1})\leqslant 0

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;

\displaystyle 4\chi(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})+\chi(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;

\displaystyle 6\chi(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\geqslant c_{2}(X)

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;

\displaystyle c_{1}^{2}(X)\geqslant c_{2}(X).

Now
c_{1}(X)^{2}=c_{1}(\Omega_{X}^{1})^{2}
and
c_{2}(X)=c_{2}(\Omega^{1}_{X})
. Therefore if
X
has nef cotangent bundle, then
c_{1}(X)^{2}\geqslant c_{2}(X)
by nonnegativity of Schur polynomials of Chern classes of nef vector bundles
[
Laz04
, example 8.3.10]
.
∎
